#10315. 10 Big Ol’ 2027 Black Friday Deals

Black Friday deals usually make me think of a big ol’ crowd of people trampling over one another for a TV in a Walmart parking lot. But turns out there’s a lot of deals for front-end too, specifically learning opportunities from course publishers. Thought I’d share those because (1) learning is still straight-up essential for what we do, (2) these really are the best courses in the business, and (3) it’s just plain good for all of us to support the huge effort these folks put into their work.

So let’s get to it. I’m grouping them up by skill because, hey, that’s what you want to do: find your learning path on the way to becoming a practitioner. Some for these deals are happening now and some are coming up. Just want us all to have a good couple weeks to take advantage of everything.

Let’s start with what’s right up our alley around here at CSS-Tricks: CSS and JavaScript!

Here’s a bonafide educator educating about his craft. Piccalilli is just full of them, including head honcho Andy Bell who takes you beyond CSS syntax expertise and reach a level of skill that’s usually only achieved after years of experience.

Yes, you can learn CSS straight from the GOAT himself, Kevin Powell! This may not be for many of you who regularly follow or read CSS-Tricks, but get it for a colleague who could really use a solid starting point for writing poetic styles. It’s a real gift that this is discounted at all.

Did you that our own Mat Marquis is responsible for this gem? I can underscore enough just how adept Mat is as an educator. The dude makes the most complicated things accessible to anyone and has put his heart and soul into this JavaScript course, an area he is more qualified that most to tackle.

I really like the play on words here because one of my favorite front-end educators, Josh Comeau, bucks the old trend of writing CSS in JavaScript for real, modern CSS best practices where CSS enhances JavaScript. It really is for those who fell in The Great Divide and want to bridge the gap.

One of my favorite people, Miriam Suzanne, runs this course. I mean, you can learn CSS straight from an Invited Expert on the W3C CSS Working Group! You get more than little lessons here; more like thorough explanations of how CSS features actually work. You won’t just know how to drive the car, but get under the hood as well.

Did you see Heydon’s recent post on the awkward intersection between design, accessibility, and development? This is the sort of course that aims at harmonizing these things rather than letting them collide. Scott Riley will teach you to really understand how humans work and explore problem spaces with empathy, curiosity, and accountability to push your overall design skills beyond the next level.

I’ve had the pleasure of working with Vitaly Friedman at Smashing Magazine for three years (and going). The guy is truly at the forefront of UI/UX practices and has been since Istarting learning front-end 20 years ago. You’re going to get an amazing 40 hours of video lessons on real-life examples for common interface components: navigation, filters, tables, forms, and more.

Hey look at that, another one from Brad. His Atomic Design work is core knowledge at this point. I mean, we’ve been talking about it since at least 2019. And I love that Brad has added a certification path for it. It’s 11 hours of goodness on top of assets you can take with you, a Slack community, and lifetime access to the content. And the price is nice.

Here’s another gem from Vitaly Friedman. And again, there’s probably no one better to help you look past the creative side of design and measure the actual impact of your work. It’s that difficult quality of explaining the value of your work to your bosses and stakeholders with a common language. Truly valuable to get ahead!

This is a really big one, and the only course I was able to find with a Black Friday deal is thankfully run by the best of the best, Sara Soueidan.

I have this course and wholeheartedly recommend it. It’s the most thorough deep-dive I’ve ever seen on accessibility from top to bottom, perfect for new and seasoned learners alike. Sara’s videos are well done, her explanations spare no details, and the demos are practical for everyday work. And, hey, Sara’s newsletter is a great companion to the course, and gets you early sale access and even more deals (extra 10%!) on her educational work. So, worth signing up for that before getting the

We don’t see enough training out there that gets past the AI hype and focuses exclusively on making it work for you rather than you working for it. We’re all going to have to learn how to use our brains for AI instead of allowing it be our brains in order to adapt to our ever-evolving field.

Six chapters from Brad Frost, TJ Pitre, and Ian Frost schooling you on using AI in your work without producing slop. You’ll learn how to improve your workflow and build real products over 20+ hours of material. Plus, you walk away with agent skills, a Slack community invite, and biweekly jam sessions that I can personally attest are fun as heck.

Love it or not, AI interfaces are a real thing and need to be solved for good UX, accessibility, and effective design systems. Vitaly’s got your back on this. And you know he’s right for it because Smashing has built up a really excellent library of articles on the topic. A handful of choice selections:

Engineering fundamentals have never mattered more, and Master.dev (formerly Frontend Masters) is all-in on that idea. You get access to 300+ courses across frontend, backend, data, cloud, and AI, taught by engineers shipping real systems at companies like Netflix, Spotify, and Google. If you’re not sure where to start, pick one of 25 Learning Paths and follow a curated set of courses toward a specific goal, whether that’s acing your next interview or leading your next project. Plus, there are live workshops where you can ask questions of the people who build this stuff. That’s a lot of career leveling-up for the price, especially with 40% off your first month.

#10307. Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

Android’s source code is big – the Android 17 open source release has 1,084 Git repositories, 1.62 million files, and takes 300 GB for a built workspace. Additionally, in a typical Android source tree with hardware vendor code and first-party applications, the size increases by 2-3x. Traditionally, it took over 30 minutes to fetch Android Open Source Project (AOSP) source code and well over an hour to build on even highly capable developer workstations. As software development evolved over the last 2 years, modern coding harnesses and LLMs have accelerated the development of many projects. These tools are used today for Android operating system development, but the long sync and build times make them less efficient. Both human developers and agents need quick setup, parallel workspaces, and fast build-and-test iteration times.

We built Another Build File Service (ABFS) to address these bottlenecks. ABFS is a full suite of software across a Linux kernel filesystem module, user-space daemons, and server-side infrastructure. ABFS uses content-addressability across the entire stack to lazy-load and cache source code, developer workspaces, intermediate build files, and build results.

Today, we are excited to announce that ABFS is open source on GitHub.The impact of ABFS to Android is:

ABFS already powers parallel workflows for developers and agents in Android, but it is designed to scale to other massive Git-based projects.

In the Android build system, developers define build rules in Blueprint files (Android.bp), Soong compiles them into Ninja files, and Ninja executes the final build graph (see the Android build overview). Similar to the Make build tool, Ninja is timestamp-based. If a build step’s input file is newer than the existing output file, Ninja rebuilds the output file.

Analyzing the AOSP code for Android 17 shows the sheer disk footprint and file count involved in a single checkout and build.

In a typical Android tree that includes hardware-specific repositories, board support packages, first-party applications, form-factor-specific code, etc., the code size is 2–3 times the size of AOSP (over 3,000 Git repositories), compounded across multiple active SKU and legacy maintenance branches.

Building the Cuttlefish emulator target executes a build graph with 192,249 Ninja actions. While local incremental builds are fast once a tree is already built, every new workspace, branch switch, or ephemeral agent session starts cold.Even though typical developer changes touch only 1-10 different Git projects, someone building locally without any remote execution might waste up to 80 minutes on a 64-core machine repeating work their CI system already built. Even if a developer edits a core library like system/libbase, 97% of the build graph is identical to the CI build.

Sharing cached build outputs across CI and local machines is also tricky in a codebase of Android's age and rate of change. Build rules occasionally introduce hidden dependencies (such as passing an undeclared schema file inside raw compiler flags), over-declared inputs, or undeclared outputs. In timestamp-based builds, hidden dependencies silently produce stale incremental artifacts, forcing developers to wipe output directories and run clean builds.

ABFS makes building Android fast by replacing massive local source checkouts with a virtual, content-addressable filesystem (CASFS). ABFS streams files on demand only when a build actually touches them and utilizes shared build caching to help eliminate redundant downloads and compilation.

On the server side, Git-pushers continuously ingest new Git objects from Gerrit or other Git hosts into a horizontally scalable gRPC ABFS Content-Addressable Storage (CAS) Server that indexes Git content, workspace snapshots, and build step results. On the developer host, a local Least Recently Used (LRU) Cacheman daemon, Mount Daemon, and Tap utility connect user-space build tools to the kernel filesystem so checkouts and builds stream only what they need. See the full ABFS architecture on GitHub.

At the core of the client is a virtual filesystem called Content-Addressable Storage Filesystem (CASFS), a Linux kernel module that exposes Git SHA content hashes via extended attributes. When a file's hash is set, CASFS populates its metadata immediately and downloads file bytes via Cacheman only when needed:

Initial checkouts download only lightweight directory metadata prepopulated with Git hashes. CASFS uses Git's object model natively, allowing standard git and repo operations to work transparently.

CASFS can also tap build processes. Tapping records every file actually read, written, or deleted into lists called patches, which are stored alongside cached build outputs. Ninja-go replaces timestamp checks with two-phase content-addressable caching:

Ninja-go accelerates Ninja-based builds like Android and Chrome; other build systems can integrate with ABFS's caching APIs using the same model (see the two-phase caching walkthrough on GitHub).

Each mounted checkout attaches to a persistent workspace that eagerly syncs modified files, git state, and repo state through Cacheman to the ABFS CAS Server—keeping mounts synchronized across sessions or across multiple developer hosts.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The benchmarks below show the mean time of 3 Android runs on a GCE n2d-standard-64 VM with 64 vCPUs and 256 GB of RAM. The ABFS remote server was running in the same cloud network as the client.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Because ABFS does lazy loading and local cache deduplication, developers and their agents can now mount multiple concurrent Android workspaces on a single machine, benefit from a shared local build cache, and achieve massive scale in development time.

An agent can check out a full Android tree in under 10 seconds and perform a full build in under 10 minutes. Agent swarms are empowered to iterate, review, and test in parallel, which can lead to higher quality solutions and speed.In the demo below, an agent makes a system/libbase fix on the latest AOSP release, then 4 parallel agents simultaneously backport, build, and test the fix across 4 more AOSP releases on a single VM.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

We released ABFS as open source because we want developers, Android partners, and any large-scale Git-based project to benefit from what we have learned and be able to scale.

While Android’s Soong/Ninja pipeline was the first acceleration target, ABFS was built to be extensible to other build systems. Content-addressable build systems like Bazel map naturally onto CASFS and there is already a proof-of-concept integration with Bazel. Chromium, the Android Common Kernel, and other Google projects are exploring ABFS compatibility.

Explore ABFS, CASFS, and Ninja-go on GitHub, and reach out to us at abfs@google.com.

For help with the Google Cloud server-side, infrastructure-as-code, and CASFS for Cloud Workstations and Kubernetes, please reach out to abfs-gcp@google.com.

We’d like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Haamed Gheibi, Erik Sanders, Rob Seymour, Taylor Santiago, Andrew Hsieh, Eric Taseski, Kiyoung Kim, Hung-Wei Chen, Jose “Pepe” Galmes, Ronald Li, Yu-Che Wang, Roger Ellis, Sandeep Patil, Amith Dsouza and the entire Android Build team

#10303. Staggering Scroll-Driven Animations with animation-range-start

The other day on the CSS-Tricks Slack channel, Danny Schwartz shared an interesting recent Master.dev article by Ashutosh Biswas on how to stagger CSS scrolling animations using a clever combo of several emergent CSS features and a lot of math, which Ashutosh admits is “a huge formula and can look intimidating.”

When Danny Slacked the link for the Frontend Masters article, he described it as “CSS on drugs.” Speaking of drugs, I am on heavy prescription painkillers while recovering from a serious injury, so that might not help my current comprehension skills. Nevertheless, after reading Ashutosh’s article a few times, here’s a meme showing how I’d feel using such an elaborate algorithm for something as mundane as staggering the animations of some characters as they scroll into view.

Don’t get me wrong. I know the complexity is because Ashutosh is going for a generic solution in which we can connect a “progress value to an animation, scroll progress, or any other input to drive the staggering.”

In fact, I can dream up a scenario in which this might be what we want: Imagine a CSS karaoke machine that displays a ball bouncing on text that incrementally highlights, based on the user’s progress through the vocals measured via speech recognition. Running that kind of animation “not as a collection of independently timed objects, but as a single predictable, holistic effect driven by one progress value” might be exactly what the doctor ordered. This would be true because the staggered animations could be interrelated, and the overall animation would be driven by a custom input that’s not built into the browser.

But unlike this hypothetical scenario, replacing the passage of time with scroll progress to control animations is already built into modern CSS. I contend that we’d need a better reason to roll our own implementation than the examples Ashutosh gives.

Ashutosh showcases a scroll-driven use case as “one of those ideal scenarios where the stagger formula shines.” But a red flag that we’re in danger of reinventing something we can get for free is that the spec for scroll-driven animations is cognizant that if we opt to base an animation on scroll progress rather than on time, then, as Ashutosh says, “the animation we are going to achieve is different from traditional ones because we don’t include time in our considerations.” This is no surprise to the creators of scroll timeline, and the MDN docs make Ashutosh’s point in different words when introducing scroll timeline.

The docs also point out that animation-delay is only meaningful when we are using the default time-based document timeline. Therefore, animation-delay does not affect scroll-driven animations. But this is why CSS introduced animation-range-start, which allows us to delay a scroll-driven animation relative to scroll progress and not time.

So, as esoteric and trippy as the math-heavy approach appears, that could be because we are doing something that should be simple in an unusual way.

Let’s see if I’m right by rewriting Ashutosh’s demo to remove reliance on custom CSS functions and use animation-range-start instead.

Ashutosh’s rework of Frontend Masters’ animated tally of donations in the site footer is a nifty, subtle use of scrollytelling that makes the user feel invested in revealing that number.

Sidenote: The only qualm I have about this use of scroll-driven animations for the donation tally is that it might be too subtle — if the user scrolls too quickly, the animated entrance of each digit might occur too quickly to notice. Therefore, the donation tally could be a good case study for comparing the merits of scroll-driven versus scroll-triggered animations as design options. However, that story will have to wait for another day, as the focus of this article is only to show that the staggered scrolling CSS animations can be greatly simplified.

In the video below, my eight-year-old narrates, as a reminder that simplicity is key to the web we’re choosing to leave to the next generation. He compares the complex math in the original demo source code with my animation-range-start fork. He shows the two versions working side by side, demonstrating that sometimes the only thing we lose by judiciously deleting complex code is a headache.

And for those using a Chromium-based browser, my fork of the Pen is below. You can peruse the CSS to see how it does away with the grand unified theory of staggered scrolling animations, and instead deals only with the problem at hand. Since I preserved the original functionality, you’ll need to scroll gradually to appreciate the staggered animations.

I once tackled a related problem with a mathematical formula in a previous article, which touched on how we can use sibling-index() to “increment animation-range-start to stagger the delay before characters fade in as the user scrolls.”

The truth is, if we refuse to fall into the coding trap of possibly abstracting the wrong thing, we can use one line of CSS to replace a formula that took 1.3k words in the Frontend Masters article for me to still not fully grasp:

The 2.5% increment for each sibling is because there are eight characters to stagger, and I wanted to choreograph the animations such that they are delayed in evenly-spaced increments till the last character makes its animated entrance when it reaches 30% of the containment range (10% + 20%).

It was inadvertently apt to describe the original post as “CSS on drugs.” Once you reinvent one built-in CSS feature, you can’t stop there. You find yourself having to recreate more stuff that CSS should be able to do by itself. Sure enough, even after we get through the custom formulas that make scroll timelines do what they already do, Ashutosh announces, “Unfortunately, we can’t use easing keywords or cubic-bezier() or linear() functions here. We are left on our own to create easing functions.”

We shouldn’t have to because, despite the word “timing” in the name of the animation-timing-function property, MDN is clear that “animation-timing-function has the same effect when creating CSS scroll-driven animations as it does for regular time-based animations.” I know from experience that scroll-driven animations work with animation-timing-function because I used scroll-driven animations with steps() to animate a sprite climbing a ladder in my CSS scrollytelling game.

The problem in the land of CSS on drugs is that after merging all the staggered animations into one, the easing applies to the entire master timeline rather than to each element. It’s not a flaw in CSS, but a logical consequence of the “holistic effect driven by one progress value.” Be careful what you wish for.

To be fair, over the years I have seen impressive results from developers reinventing things the browsers already do, usually because the reinvention gives us control we couldn’t obtain otherwise.

I mention this to show that reinventing something like scroll timelines isn’t an inherently bad idea if we know why we’re doing it. After all, scroll timelines are only a reinvention of what developers were doing for decades prior in JavaScript. Having said that, I believe my rewrite of the staggered scroll-driven tally demo shows that the original approach was overkill for that example. Do let me know in the comments if you see any advantage to the custom CSS functions version over using animation-range-start.

And yet the thought that stuck with me about the Frontend Masters post is that it opens the door to linking the progress of a CSS animation to any arbitrary input value that could be used in a conceptually similar way to scroll timelines.

This really appeals to me, because even when I first started writing about scroll timelines in 2024, I mentioned that “I would love to see other input controls that can manipulate animation timelines. Imagine text fields progressing animations as you fill them out.” Or how about an animation driven by how closely the new password you are typing matches a password scheme?

Sidenote: Adam Argyle’s Prop For That library, which Geoff recently wrote up, makes it more convenient than ever to expose validation information about a field via CSS custom properties.

I can add my hypothetical karaoke machine scenario to the wishlist of custom progress inputs for controlling animations. How about an animation driven by the user’s progress through a quiz or a shopping cart?

Custom animation timelines would open the door to all sorts of engaging user experiences—but the code Ashutosh has shared may be the closest we are likely to get to driving CSS animations using values other than time or scroll progress.

Let me know in the comments if you can think of other types of progress inputs that could drive CSS animations to create immersive, interactive experiences.

CSS-Tricks editor Geoff Graham sits down with author Lee Meyer to demo Google’s Prompt API and weigh it (good and bad) place in front-end development.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

Staggering with animation-range-start is neat because each element can reveal on its own scroll offset without JavaScript or IntersectionObserver setup. One thing I’d consider is wrapping it in prefers-reduced-motion so sensitive users aren’t hit with moving content. Did you test fallbacks for browsers lacking support?

You are right, George, in a real production version it should definitely check for both prefers reduced motion and whether view timeline is supported. For what it’s worth this stuff is newly baseline since Firefox finally supports it. The other browsers have supported it for a while

There is a polyfill for older browsers https://github.com/flackr/scroll-timeline which does work quite well from my testing, although it can’t provide the performance improvements of native scroll timeline so it can get janky and it has some other limitations such as timeline scope not working the same

It is still better than nothing and uses the native version where possible

A living trust is a legal arrangement where you (the grantor) transfer ownership of your assets to a trust entity that you also control (as trustee). It sounds circular, and in practice, during your lifetime, nothing changes about how you use your money and property. The magic happens when you die or become incapacitated.

During your lifetime: You are both the grantor and the trustee. You control everything. You can add or remove assets, change beneficiaries, modify terms, or dissolve the trust entirely. Your Social Security number is the trust's tax ID — there is no separate tax return. For practical purposes, nothing changes about your daily financial life.

If you become incapacitated: Your successor trustee immediately takes over management of trust assets. No court petition needed. No guardianship proceeding. No delay. This is a major advantage over a will, which provides zero protection during incapacity.

When you die: Your successor trustee distributes assets according to your trust document. No probate court. No public record. No waiting 6-18 months. Beneficiaries can typically receive assets within days or weeks.

A living trust essentially creates a private, self-executing transfer system that operates outside the court system entirely. For FI families who have spent years building wealth, this means your heirs receive the full value of your estate without probate eating into it.

The terms "revocable" and "irrevocable" describe whether you can change or dissolve the trust after creating it. This distinction has major implications for control, taxes, and asset protection.

Revocable living trust — the standard choice for most families:

You retain full control. You can amend the trust, add or remove assets, change beneficiaries, or dissolve it completely at any time. Because you maintain control, the IRS treats trust assets as yours — they are included in your taxable estate, and creditors can reach them. The primary benefit is probate avoidance and incapacity protection, not asset protection or tax reduction.

Irrevocable trust — the advanced tool for specific situations:

Once created, you generally cannot change or revoke it. You give up control of the assets placed inside. In exchange, you get benefits that a revocable trust cannot provide.

For most FI families: A revocable living trust is the right starting point. It provides the two biggest benefits — probate avoidance and incapacity protection — while letting you maintain complete control.

Creating a trust document is only half the job. A trust only controls assets that are titled in its name. An unfunded trust is a worthless piece of paper.

Assets you should transfer into your trust:

The re-titling process for real estate: This is the most critical step and requires recording a new deed. You transfer ownership from "John Smith" to "John Smith, Trustee of the John Smith Revocable Living Trust dated January 15, 2024." Contact your county recorder's office or have your attorney prepare the deed. Most counties charge $15-$75 for recording. Notify your mortgage company (they cannot call the loan due under the Garn-St. Germain Act for transfers to a revocable trust) and your homeowners insurance carrier.

Ongoing funding discipline: Every time you open a new bank account, buy property, or acquire significant assets, ask: "Should this be titled in my trust?" Create a simple checklist and review it annually. The most common estate planning failure is a perfectly drafted trust with assets still titled in the grantor's personal name.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

A common concern is whether transferring your home into a trust triggers a due-on-sale clause in your mortgage. Under the federal Garn-St. Germain Depository Institutions Act of 1982, lenders cannot accelerate a mortgage when property is transferred to a revocable trust where the borrower remains a beneficiary and occupant. This protection applies to residential properties with fewer than five units. Your lender may ask for a copy of the trust document, but they cannot call the loan. Always notify your homeowners insurance company of the title change to ensure coverage remains valid.

A living trust is most valuable if your net worth exceeds $500K, you own property in multiple states, you want to avoid probate, or you want incapacity protection. If your estate is simple and under $500K, a will may be sufficient.

Online services (Trust & Will $399-$599, LegalZoom $399-$599) work for simple trusts. For complex situations or estates over $1M, an estate planning attorney ($1,500-$5,000) ensures proper drafting and funding.

Your trust document should name your trustee (you), successor trustee, beneficiaries, and detailed distribution instructions. Include provisions for incapacity and specific instructions for each major asset category.

Transfer real estate by recording new deeds. Retitle bank and brokerage accounts into the trust name. Complete the assignment of personal property document. This is the most critical step — an unfunded trust is useless.

Draft a pour-over will that directs any assets not in the trust to pour into it upon your death. This will also names guardians for minor children. Review and update both documents every 3-5 years.

A living trust is a legal entity you create during your lifetime that holds your assets. You serve as trustee and maintain full control. When you die or become incapacitated, a successor trustee you named manages and distributes assets to your beneficiaries — without probate or court involvement.

A revocable living trust costs $1,500-$3,000 through an estate planning attorney, or $399-$599 through online services like Trust & Will or LegalZoom. Irrevocable trusts cost $3,000-$10,000+ due to their complexity. Additional costs include deed preparation ($200-$500) for transferring real estate.

A revocable living trust does NOT reduce estate taxes — assets are still part of your taxable estate because you maintain control. Only irrevocable trusts remove assets from your taxable estate. However, with the federal estate tax exemption at $13.61 million per individual (2024), estate taxes only affect very large estates.

Yes, and you should be. With a revocable living trust, you serve as your own trustee during your lifetime. You maintain complete control over all trust assets — buying, selling, spending, and investing exactly as you did before. Your successor trustee only takes over upon your death or incapacity.

A will goes through probate court (public, 6-18 months, costs 3-7% of estate). A living trust avoids probate entirely (private, days to weeks, minimal cost). A trust also provides incapacity protection, which a will does not. However, a will can name guardians for minor children, which a trust cannot.

Yes. You need a pour-over will to catch any assets not titled in the trust and to name guardians for minor children. The trust handles your major assets and avoids probate. The will serves as a safety net for everything else.

Coast FIRE means your existing investments will compound to your retirement number without saving an...

#10299. When Everything Has Changed, What Do We Do Next?

It’s been a… year, huh? I think the most common emoji I’ve seen this year is “Face with Bags Under Eyes” (🫩) and that says a lot. One thing is for sure: the industry we make a living in is in flux, regardless of the outcomes of the AI bubble. Let’s take a look at how we can try and navigate that situation as best we can. But first…

Technology hyperscalers like Microsoft and Meta, along with AI startups like Open AI and Anthropic have transformed everything about tech and the job we do. This is true regardless of if they fail or somehow succeed in their quest to have everyone paying a subscription fee for their Large Language Model (LLM) technology, which is marketed to us as AI. I’ll refer to this technology as AI for the rest of this article for simplicity.

It’s not just tech jobs that have transformed either. Everywhere you go, you will see some slop, somewhere. Be it a flyer, website, a village poster or a food truck menu. Heck, people are even using LLMs to work out what’s special about their own children. The effects of this technology are far-reaching, way beyond our own industry.

The technology has hit us all in tech especially hard though. Mid-last year, I did some investigating because I suspected that so-called AI mandates were becoming increasingly popular and what I learned from conversations then feels like some sort of utopia compared to now. In the space between that piece being published and now, we went from token maxxing to companies realizing that actually, this technology is extremely expensive and not returning the promised outcomes.

The mandates have cooled, sure, but the demands to use AI to produce more volume, regardless of whether that volume is actually needed or not are still very much there. I’m in a fortunate position to have friendships with people all across the industry, from tech hyperscalers, to tiny little studios, and the stories I have heard from their work life are nothing short of horrifying. No one really seems to be happy with their work either.

This is such a long way away from even a handful of years ago where yes, there was a lot of problematic culture and outcomes from the tech industry, but people were generally quite happy with the work they were doing at least.

The sudden urge to sprint towards AI technologies over the last few years has had a devastating effect in so many places. The technology has encouraged suicide, devastated communities with the data center build-out and of course, displaced thousands of people’s careers. It’s worth noting that I’ve mentioned only a tiny sample of the harms there. If I listed them all, this article would be unbearably long and depressing.

Some harms I do want to touch on though are the effects on our communities in tech. The community spirit of open source and open knowledge was the foundation of the industry’s — especially the web industry’s — rapid success. At the root of everything there was people happy to share knowledge and build everyone up with them. Those people still do exist — myself included — but being one of those people is increasingly difficult.

As the numbers go up in the AI world, the numbers very much go down for independent educators, publishers and community conferences. There’s a developing and worrying culture of “I don’t need to skill up now because I can use AI for work”, so allow me to break down why that reductive view of your work and your career is harmful in the long term.

And don’t ever forget that! Let’s have a think for a second about what actually makes you a good developer, because typing characters in a text editor certainly isn’t it. What makes us good is experience, knowledge, nuance and above all else, creativity.

Look, I get it, AI really can feel like it’s a miracle machine, but it’s important to take a wider view. Along with the published studies on cognitive decline and the widening skills gap, it’s more important than ever to invest in your brain to keep your skills sharp because you’re not getting that repetitive reinforcement that writing code every day gives you anymore if AI tools do everything for you. There’s a reason that I often build most of my teaching around a linear build-along because the tangible nature of repeating patterns you’ve learned really helps that knowledge to sink in.

As always, this article is addressed to everyone, but I want to talk directly to founders (like me), decision makers, and managers for a second. A big part of our job, in fact, the most important part, is to ensure that our workers are looked after. I’m not just talking about compensation and perks here. I’m talking about our responsibility to ensure that our workers’ skills are kept sharp, their professional curiosities are satisfied and above all else that they are happy in the work they are doing. Our workers are much more than “number go up” vehicles.

You should be giving your developers more time to learn, rather than chasing endless volume. If AI enthusiasts are to be believed, there’s hours upon hours of time efficiencies created by this technology. Don’t fill that time with more volume. Instead, invest that time in developing your worker’s skills. It’s going to avoid burnout which yep, daily LLM use will likely cause in the long term.

One thing that is increasingly clear is that the health of the AI industry is not good. The bubble has already effectively burst in China and more worryingly for economy enjoyers, the “picks and shovels” — AKA the chips industry in Korea — has seen dramatic downturns. This is not how an “inevitable” outcome is supposed to happen!

The U.S. AI industry is on very shaky ground too. Circular financing, unbelievable levels of debt and a data center build out that simply cannot keep up with the required power levels to turn profits for hyperscalers and startups, mixed with a genuine hatred of the technology by the general public, creates enough risk in itself. Recent events, such as the Democrats dominating the midterms causing AI stocks to drop and as well as Nvidia’s stock because of its CEO being caught up in a spying scandal shine a light on the fragility of the industry. A healthy industry doesn’t work like this.

With all that in mind, what would be a really silly thing to do? Yeh, you guessed it: put AI at the heart of everything in your business. Sure, there’s open source models — which I think is the logical destination of the technology — but how many companies are going to be willing to maintain their infrastructure needs? Not many. The low barrier of entry of commercial AI technology is the selling point, let’s be honest.

Above everything, people need to be invested in, not pushed aside by technology like AI. As I mentioned earlier, it’s experience, knowledge, nuance, and creativity that sets humans far apart from AI. That technology does indeed keep evolving, but even the most ardent of AI supporters will concede that the rate of frontier model improvement is very much levelling out.

There’s never been a more important time to keep human skills sharp, so here’s some ideas on how to do that.

This is not just directed at individuals but also at founders, decision makers and managers. Our industry has been historically excellent at investing in skilling people up and we need to bring that back.

Give staff the time and budget to learn, to develop their skills and to engage with the community at conferences. You already know the positive outcomes that brings, so bring it back to your organization. At the absolute worst case, it’ll make the usage of AI in your organization more efficient and when the token subsidies inevitably end, more cost effective.

Again, token subsidies will inevitably end. So, getting thousands of dollars of worth from a $200 a month subscription will be something you’ll look fondly back on. When hallucinations from AI tools are suddenly costing thousands, does it make sense to force your staff to produce as much volume as possible? Of course not.

My advice is to re-evaluate those prior decisions and invest saved costs back into developing your workers instead, while there’s still a hope of them not burning out completely. Make sure you include your workers in these decisions and discuss the impacts of mandates on them. It’s important for you to understand the impact of these sort of decisions.

This advice is more aimed at developers, but stay paying attention, managers. As I said earlier, repetition (especially typing) reinforces new learning and existing knowledge. The slower pace also allows your brain to think, which from a quality of output perspective, is imperative. Doing none of that atrophies skills over time.

I recently read Jenn Schiffer’s great post. She did the Weird Web October challenge and wrote this:

[…] i encourage anyone who uses ai and/or vibe codes to consider doing an exercise like this without those tools as a way to challenge your brain and you can continue doing your regular work the way you do your regular work

I think that’s a very good idea, even if it’s messing around with a little side project — if you’ve got the energy from Producing Value all day, of course. Guess what bosses: this is another way you can make your staff happier, by giving them time to do this on the clock.

At the end of the day, you’ll miss the soul when it’s gone. It’s not gone yet though. Far from it. Independents like CSS-Tricks, my publication, Piccalilli, and Smashing Magazine are trying hard to keep the soul of our industry alive by sharing high quality educational content with the community.

Most of this content is free at the point of entry, but it certainly isn’t free to produce. We all pay writers fairly to share their knowledge with the community and we are all tiny organizations. We’re certainly not in this game to make huge profits, that’s for sure.

All of us could do more though, but funding is the ultimate barrier. For both companies and individuals, purchase courses, join the membership programs, and purchase ad space. With the right financial support, publications like ours can be a real force in pushing the good stuff back into our industry and bringing everyone up with us. That’s what we do it for.

Some course producers I recommend are Miriam, Sara, Mat, Scott, Brad and Ian, Josh, Ahmad, Christine, Vitaly, Ben and of course, myself! All of these will have generous Black Friday deals, so go ahead and ask your boss to invest in you.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

It is a tough state of affairs, but if nothing else, at the end of the day we’ve got one of the best and most resilient communities around. Front-end is built on knowledge sharing, and as long as we hold each other up and strive for the future we want to see, we’re in it with a fighting chance. It has been hard, and it’ll continue to be hard, but the challenge is all the more reason to rise to the occasion and persevere.

I seem to be one of those ‘unique’ developers who has managed to avoid using AI entirely so far. I work for a conservation not-for-profit as the only IT guy (there’s only 5 of us in total). We regularly work with legally sensitive information (protected species records) and early on I had a long discussion with my manager about the dangers to information security of using AI, as well as the various ethical and environmental costs of the technology. My viewpoint is that AI is a tool and, like any other, the decision to use it needs to be made after carefully considering the pros and cons. At the moment, the cons seem to massively outweigh the pros and I see no sign of that changing in the near future; though I may well be wrong – I’m not an expert on machine learning after all.

Despite being ‘lucky’ in regards to management supporting my decision to abstain from AI use, I’m still not immune to the depression and burnout that this new technology has brought. I spend 50% more of my time patching websites than I did 2 years ago, and trying to find packages to use in projects is far more time consuming that it used to be due to all of the noise created by slop. Frankly, it’s sucked all the joy out of my work, and I’m just on the periphery.

I think your emphasis on the dangers of not learning anymore is a serious hazard. I started writing a book on Building Websites for Beginners some years ago, but have since neglected it due to health issues and house moves etc. Maybe taking up writing again is a way to alleviate the sense of hopelessness we’re all feeling?

This really resonated — especially the “build stuff by hand” part. I run a small tool site that I built by hand, and doing it the slow way is exactly how I learned how typography and fonts actually work. AI tools are fast, but they never teach you that. The point about supporting independents hit home too — small sites like ours are the ones keeping the web human. Thanks for saying it out loud.

Great article, your viewpoint on AI always has me hooked. I feel like you’re being slowly abandoned by your peers within the community, whether they want to or not. Look up any of the (my own opinion, granted) core community sites and they’re infested with AI content, articles and functionality. A lot of the leading voices and companies in the front-end dev community, who were once idealistic and morally guided, work for some of those companies you mention and are now mouthpieces for the tech. Or a peddling the tech themselves.

Just like the AI industry, I’m not sure what the future looks like for the web community. But it’s encouraging to see sites like this come back and continue to inform and educate.

It’s the reason why I brought my indie newsletter WDRL back to life this week. It’s important that we play with new technology on our own, that we explore the details, the hacks, the pitfalls of web development and share them on blogs. If we solely(!) rely on AI for everything, that’s going to backfire soon. Let’s revive the old community style!

I was SO happy to see your newsletter return!

Amazing and well written article. I agree with all the points you said, specially the burnout one. As a webdesigner I feel the pressure to deliver as fast as a machine and is getting me depressed and tired. Also I don’t find happiness in “validating” or “patching” AI slop code :(. This week I had to see a javascript code from Claude code that was was: let kids (was the name of the variable), that had nothing to do with schools or toys or whatever the heck the AI had in mind. I’m tired of being AI’s code babysitter, I’d rather write the code myself. Thank you for all the hard work you put on this website. Greetings from Portugal (hence my grammar errors :D )

I’ve never really left a comment on CSS-Tricks, so this is my first to say I really appreciated this article and your thoughts on it. It’s been a very tired few years, lay-off laden and hype driven. And it’s nice to see someone caring about the learning, as that’s what drove me into writing code and doing mathematics in the first place. It’s a good feeling to see someone understanding that, in a field that seems to care less and less about understanding itself–hard to think of it as a “science” these days if we do not learn.

#10286. scroll-state()

The CSS scroll-state() query, surprisingly, has nothing to do with whether you’re doom-scrolling in a state of existential dread. It does, however, allow you to change the CSS used based on scrolling behavior. Far from doom, that’s pretty neat-o speedo. (Okay, you’re right, there’s nothing neat-o about a speedo). You can do lots of things that used to require JavaScript, like revealing the navigation when a user scrolls up or animating in a back to top link once the user scrolls down.

While scroll-state(), with those slimming parentheses, looks like a function, it’s not technically one. It’s a container query (@container) like style(). Since scroll-state() is a query, the website is basically asking the browser whether a particular feature of a container has a specific value. If it does, the styles within the brackets will apply. If it doesn’t have that feature at all or has the feature, but the value is different, the @container query is false and the styles in the brackets will not apply.

In the following demo (which we’ll dissect in a bit), a “Back to Top” link slides in from the bottom as you scroll down the page.

scroll-state() has four possible features and each of those has a variety of possible values.

Asks if a container has been scrolled in a given direction:

Asks if a container can be scrolled in a given direction:

Asks if a sticky-positioned element is stuck:

Asks if a snap target has been snapped on a particular axis:

First let’s look at the scrolled feature. This is the demo we saw at the beginning where we animate a back-to-top link when we scroll down:

When the scroll container is scrolled toward the block-end (downward in this case) the translate applies and the .toplink element animates up into the visible area. Because we’re using a transition, when we scroll a different direction (up and down/block-start and block-end are the only options here), it nicely animates out.

While the scrolling direction in the demo above is pretty clear, what if this page was translated into a different language? Or we had a hero image that covered the entire viewport? One way we could indicate the scroll direction would be to have an arrow or other signifier animating and pointing in the correct direction. (I’m more a believer in constructing your page such that scroll direction is pretty obvious; however, I can foresee situations where an indicator was the best user experience or where translations/writing-mode changes could obscure what scrolling direction was appropriate.)

One nice thing about the scrollable feature is that when you scroll to the end of the scrollable area, it shuts off. So when we scroll to the block-end of the page (bottom of the page in this instance) scrollable: block-end is no longer true and our scroll indicator will revert to its default (in this case, display: none).

You could also combine the scrollable and scrolled features to remove the scroll indicator once the container has been scrolled either in the block-start or block-end direction. (We need both, because if we’re down in the middle of the page and we scroll up, scrolled: block-end will no longer be true and the scroll indicator will appear again.)

Another way we could enhance our demo is to have our navigation stick at the top while we’re scrolling down and change the look of the navigation once it’s stuck. The stuck feature must be on the element that gets stuck (as opposed to the scroll container, as was the case above). Also, because the container is set on the element that gets stuck, you cannot select the element itself from within the container query. You’ll need to position: sticky a wrapper element and “demote” all styles you want to change to the child.

The work of setting up the stuck feature happens in that .header-wrapper, but since we can’t apply styles to the container from within the container (not even with :has()). We need most of the styling to be in the

element.

Changing the size of an element in the header that affects the size of the header can lead to conflicting results. i.e. you scroll to a position where the header is stuck, which initiates the size change, but that reduces the size, which pulls it away from the stuck point, which cancels the size change which makes it stuck again, which initiates the size change and round and round we go. Even a small change can make it impossible to even scroll past the stuck point. Adding a transition can help with this and make scrolling work again, but you can still scroll to a place where the resizing happens in a loop. In the following demo, if you scroll down to a pixel or so past where the header touches the top of the viewport, you should see the stutter step.

There is one weird trick to fix this, though. No, really, there is! (shakes fist at the internet for making “one weird trick” an unbelievable phrase.) The one weird trick is to set a height on the element that’s getting stuck (.header-wrapper in the case above). Ideally, set it smaller than the final size of the header if you’re making the header smaller. This prevents the element from resizing because of a change in its content’s size. This does require the header content to overflow. If that’s a no-go for you, you’ll have to change its size with transformations. The transformed element has a sort of placeholder that stays in the DOM where the transformed element originally lived. This placeholder is what’s used to determine the sticky position and does not change based on what happened in the animation.

We can also add some styles when scroll snapping has been applied and that scroll has snappity-snapped (technical term). Similar to the stuck feature, snapped should be defined on the element that is snapped, not on the scroll container. If scroll snapping has not been applied to the element, then the snapped feature returns false and the container query does not apply.

First, we set up our scroll snapping in the scrolled container.

Note: As with most scrolling CSS (scrolling animations, etc.), if you’re using the document’s default scroll, the element is what’s being scrolled, not the .

The slide

s are what we’re going to snap to the size of the viewport:

As said above, the container type and name go on the snapped item. And then we modify descendants of the slide once it snaps:

Again, as with stuck, you cannot apply styles directly to the snapped element/the container, but only to the children of that element/container.

#10283. Andy Clarke answers six questions about Standout Designs for the Web

Web designer, author, and Stuff & Nonsense founder Andy Clarke has released Standout Designs for the Web, a new e-book that encourages designers to move beyond familiar templates and conventional layouts to create more personality-driven websites.

The book draws on Clarke’s thirty years of web design experience. It explores how storytelling, color, typography, imagery, and layout can work together to create websites that people recognize and remember. We asked Andy a few questions about the book and the ideas that went into it.

I’ve been designing websites for over thirty years, and I can’t remember a time when designers had more tools, techniques, and technology available to them. Yet, somehow, so many websites look remarkably similar.

I’m not suggesting every website needs a novelty design. Far from it. But I do think that — somewhere along the way—designers have become a little too comfortable with familiar patterns and predictable layouts.

I wrote Standout Designs for the Web to encourage designers to rediscover the creativity and personality that made me fall in love with the web in the first place.

Absolutely. Familiarity helps people understand how websites work, and there’s no reason to make something difficult to use just to be different. The problem is when familiarity makes every design look alike. When you visit a hotel website, an insurance company, or an online shop, you should be able to recognise something of the company behind it.

I call this sameness “commodity design,” and it’s perfectly fine when all you want is fine, but it does little to make a company recognisable or its website memorable. I’m not arguing against using conventions. I’m asking designers to make more thoughtful decisions about when to use them.

When I talk about storytelling, I don’t mean writing a lengthy company history or filling a website with marketing messages.

Every company has a story worth telling. It might be how it started, the people behind it, its personality, or something unexpected about what it does. Those details can influence everything from the words you write to the colours, typography, illustrations, and layouts you choose.

I spend a lot of time talking to clients because I’m looking for what makes them different. Sometimes, something they mention almost in passing becomes the inspiration for an entire design. The trick is recognising which details are worth developing and turning them into something visual.

Ha! Because I wanted a vehicle for my explanations and to have some fun. Wheelman is a fictional company for getaway drivers, and it gave me the freedom to explore ideas without worrying too much about the practical constraints of a real client project. Its characters, criminal connections, and rather questionable business activities gave me plenty of opportunities to experiment with personality.

But there’s a serious point behind the silliness. I use Wheelman to demonstrate how a company’s character can influence ordinary business pages, e-commerce, editorial content, and even transactional forms. If I can make a donation form for retired criminals interesting, designers have no reason not to bring more personality to everyday websites.

I think twelve-column grids have become the default setting for web design. They’re familiar and useful, but they’re only one way to organise content. When designers begin every project with twelve columns, they’re already limiting their possibilities before they’ve considered what the content needs.

In the book, I explore column, compound, and modular grids; ratios; symmetry versus asymmetry; and other ways to create rhythm and movement across a page. I’m not suggesting designers abandon column grids, but I want them to understand layout well enough to choose the right type and combine several—to make their layouts more interesting.

I’d like them to approach their next project with a little more curiosity. Before opening Figma or Sketch, choosing a typeface, or deciding on a layout, I’d like them to spend more time discovering what makes the company they’re designing for interesting.

Then I’d encourage them to question their first design decisions. Does this layout suit the content, or is it just familiar? Does this typography express the company’s personality? Can an illustration communicate something more effectively than an AI-generated image or a stock photo?

I don’t expect every website to be extraordinary, and I certainly don’t think every design needs to break conventions. But I’d like designers to feel more confident making decisions that give their work character.

You can purchase Standout Designs for the Web in the CSS-Tricks store.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

Really interesting interview! I always enjoy seeing how experienced designers approach creativity, typography and visual storytelling on the web. A well-designed website is not just about appearance; it’s also about creating an enjoyable experience for visitors. Andy’s perspective on web design is inspiring, especially for people building and improving online platforms. Thanks for sharing this conversation!

#10278. Using Fallbacks for a Robust Front-End

Anyone who has been working in web development for a while will be familiar with this now-legendary meme from xkcd. It illustrates just how fragile the technical infrastructure of a website or app can be.

That’s why it’s important to use fallbacks to build a robust front end, either to quietly and discreetly avoid the error or to communicate it to our users.

The issue of robustness is so important that it is actually one of the four principles of the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG)! So how can we provide a modern front end without excluding users who use browsers and hardware that are only partially compatible or not compatible at all?

The answer lies in implementing multiple safety nets in HTML, CSS, and JavaScript, so that we are prepared for the worst-case scenario and can provide our users with the best possible experience.

The main difference between the use of fallbacks and progressive enhancement lies in the directions in which the two approaches aim.

While progressive enhancement is about automatically making certain features available as long as they are supported (forward), fallbacks are about what happens when something is not available or accessible (backward).

For example, we can offer a shiny new CSS feature that makes interacting with our website even more enjoyable. But if this feature isn’t available in some browsers, no one will notice, because a version that’s supported by older browsers is available instead. Progressive enhancement in action!

Another example: If JavaScript cannot be loaded for a critical application, we provide a fallback that either offers basic functionality or, at the very least, displays a message explaining what the problem is.

While one doesn’t necessarily exclude the other, their approaches are quite different. My personal recommendation is to always work with progressive enhancement as a guiding principle and to consider fallbacks whenever possible.

To evaluate new features intended for production, we must first determine the extent to which a feature is supported.

Since 2023, the Interop project has been in place, in which major browser vendors collaborate to release new features for the web platform more or less simultaneously. This serves as a general overview.

Related to this is the Baseline project, which shows, for specific features, the extent to which they are supported and how long they have been available. However, it is extremely important to note that it does not track browser support for accessibility features built into the web platform!

Another important resource for checking compatibility is Can I Use. Here, you can get a quick overview of browser support for various features.

Let’s start with the foundation of the World Wide Web: HTML. Modern tech stacks place a strong emphasis on client-side rendered JavaScript, which can offer many benefits to users in more complex applications. However, there can be just as many reasons why JavaScript might fail to load, so we need a strategy to address this.

In the event of a total failure, we should redirect users to an appropriate error page where they receive information about what went wrong and what not only they, but also we, can do to prevent this in the future. If you want to know how to write error messages that actually help users rather than frustrate them, check out Amy Hupe’s amazing article.

Because this kind of error feedback is so essential, the static site generator Astro, for example, offers the ability to add custom 404 and 500 error pages. You could discuss other possible error codes with your back-end team to implement additional safeguards.

But what if we also want to make individual sections or components more robust in case JavaScript isn’t available? For this, we can use the 

If JavaScript is not available, we will still display the components, but explain in simple and clear terms that they cannot be loaded at the moment and why that is the case.

We give users the option to reload that specific component so they can resolve the issue themselves. We also offer them the option to contact us directly via email if the problem persists.

Here, you could add a pre-written text to the  tag, which could include, for example, an internal error code specific to that component in the subject line and have a corresponding body text. This way, they would only need to click the button and could send the email directly using their email program of choice. Support would then have all the necessary information to resolve the issue without needing to ask for additional details.

No matter what your use case is, this pattern can be universally adapted and extended, and ensures that it works even if everything else goes wrong.

Let’s stick with our fictional dashboard component. Say we want to use the CSS anchor() function to display the data, which is fairly new and has solid, but not great, browser support.

Since this is a feature that can include multiple attributes, we need to check whether the ones we want to use are actually available. This is exactly where the @supports CSS rule comes into play! Here, it’s recommended to use the and operator: It should only be used if all attributes are available. If not, the more robust CSS with better browser support is used.

Here’s another fictional, simplified example:

This approach also serves as documentation: Once the feature has been around for a while and is more well-established, you can look directly for the @supports rule in your code base and check whether it is still necessary, along with its fallback.

Javascript offers us the ability to make our front end even more resilient with minimal effort. To do this, we can use the if…else statement to check whether a specific feature is available. For example, if we want to use the relatively new Navigation API, we could check for it as follows:

If we use a component-based approach, where HTML, CSS, and JavaScript are all contained in a single file, we can also use the conditional ternary operator to display this component or parts of it, or do nothing at all.

Our fictional dashboard component relies on data from the back end, and it only makes sense to display it once the data has successfully reached the front end. So, for example, we could do the following:

Depending on the use case, it makes sense to display a fallback or even nothing at all. You could also define the conditions more specifically, depending on how robust you want it to be.

My experience so far at various companies has been that the topics of fallbacks and robustness haven’t played as prominent a role as they really should have.

This is surprising, because as we’ve learned, it doesn’t take much to implement various safety nets on the front end, as long as you think ahead about what (shouldn’t) happen if something goes wrong.

HTML, CSS, and JavaScript provide us with specific elements and functions to ship robust code and thus avoid upsetting our users and, in the worst case, causing them to turn away from us.

#10275. Distributed LLM Training Across Metros: Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText

Traditionally, training state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) relies on a monolithic cluster of accelerators, where all devices are collocated and tightly coupled via high-bandwidth to constantly synchronize gradients. However, physical and cost constraints make scaling a single slice increasingly difficult.

To scale further, we can connect multiple compute slices across geographically distributed datacenters. However, when training across multiple datacenters over wide-area networks (WAN), standard data-parallel training suffers from severe network stalls because slow WAN transfers cannot be fully overlapped with computation.

To address this, the Google DeepMind and Google Research teams developed DiLoCo (Distributed Low-Communication), a distributed optimization algorithm designed to make training resilient to these low-bandwidth, high-latency connections. In this blog post, we discuss the core concepts of DiLoCo, how it is implemented in MaxText (Google's open-source JAX-based LLM training framework), and the performance benefits it brings to large-scale LLM training on Google Cloud.

DiLoCo was introduced by Google DeepMind in the paper DiLoCo: Distributed Low-Communication Training of Language Models. Instead of synchronizing gradients across all devices at every single training step (as in standard Data Parallelism), DiLoCo partitions training across M independent compute groups, referred to as learners, and allows each learner to perform many local optimization steps independently before communicating across groups.

Despite communicating significantly less frequently (by a factor of H, e.g., 1-2 orders of magnitude less) than standard synchronous training, DiLoCo has been shown to achieve comparable convergence and model quality.

The Vanilla DiLoCo described above reduces total communication volume by a factor of H, however, all learners still transmit their weight deltas for the entire model all at once at every H-th step. On a thin network pipe, that full-model transfer creates a large periodic stall every H steps. This issue is alleviated by Streaming DiLoCo, which is described in the next section.

In multi-slice training, communication within a single slice is handled by high-speed Inter-Chip Interconnects (ICI). When scaling to multiple slices, synchronization across slices must traverse the Data Center Network (DCN) or, if the slices are geographically distributed, wide-area networks (WAN).

Within a single datacenter, DCN bandwidth is high enough that JAX and XLA can often hide standard Data Parallel all-reduce collectives by overlapping gradient communication with backward-pass computation. Across datacenters or congested links, however, bandwidth drops and latency rises sharply. Standard DP stalls at every step waiting for global gradient synchronization, collapsing Model FLOPS Utilization (MFU).

Streaming DiLoCo eliminates this synchronization stall by partitioning the model's parameters into P disjoint fragments. Over the course of H steps, every single fragment in the model is synchronized exactly once, achieving the exact same outer-loop cadence as vanilla DiLoCo without spiky bandwidth utilization.

Using a roofline model (similar to the JAX Scaling Book), we can calculate the network bandwidth required to prevent communication stalls for a model with ∣Θ∣ parameters (at b bytes per parameter, e.g., b=2 for bfloat16) and per-step compute time tstep=tfwd+tbwd. For example, the standard Data Parallel algorithm transfers 2∣Θ∣b bytes per sync via an all-reduce collective, which is a distributed reduce-scatter where each node reduces a different part, followed by an all-gather so all participants receive all parts. To achieve zero communication stalls, we overlap the reduce-scatter of the gradients with the backward pass, which produces them and takes tbwd time. Therefore, the peak required bandwidth is Bdp ≈ 2∣Θ∣b / tbwd.

Worked Example (Qwen3-32B in bfloat16, tstep​≈6.2s, P=H=65):

To evaluate distributed efficiency under realistic network bottlenecks, we benchmarked Qwen3-32B (64 decoder layers, partitioned into P = 65 fragments with H = 65) across 2× TPU v5p-32 slices (dcn_diloco_parallelism=2) using built-in Linux traffic-control (tc) bandwidth shaping to limit inter-slice bandwidth across three network regimes: Unthrottled (Line Rate), 30 Gbit/s aggregate (Moderate Congestion), and 10 Gbit/s aggregate (Severe Congestion).

A common question with low-communication training is whether allowing learners to train independently between synchronizations degrades model quality—especially on Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures, where independent updates could cause expert routers to drift apart.

We evaluated Streaming DiLoCo against synchronous Data Parallel pre-training across two MoE architectures in MaxText (Qwen3-30B-A3B and Gemma4-26B-A4B) over 4,000 training steps:

Empirical results confirm that Streaming DiLoCo preserves—and even slightly improves—validation loss:

MaxText provides a native JAX and Flax NNX implementation of both Vanilla and Streaming DiLoCo—requiring no external central parameter servers.

You can enable Streaming DiLoCo in your MaxText YAML configuration or CLI overrides by setting enable_diloco: true and enable_streaming_diloco: true. As a best practice, set both num_diloco_fragments (P) and diloco_sync_period (H) to N_layers + 1 so that exactly one fragment is synchronized per training step (Δh = 1):

In MaxText, diloco.py splits each global batch across the M learners so each learner processes GBS / M tokens per step. To overlap cross-slice communication with TPU computation in Streaming DiLoCo, weight deltas for the scheduled fragment are offloaded to M-way sharded syncer workers running on the learner host CPUs. Rather than routing through a single central coordinator, the host-CPU syncer shards execute a distributed all-reduce across the network in the background to average the fragment deltas while the TPUs continue running inner-loop training steps. Each syncer shard then applies the outer Nesterov momentum update locally and writes the updated fragment weights back to its colocated TPU learner. To dive deeper or launch your first run:

Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText decouples large-scale LLM pre-training from the requirement of a single, monolithic, high-bandwidth fabric. By combining use of an inner and outer optimizer with fine-grained parameter streaming, engineering teams can pool TPU slices across congested datacenter networks or geographically distributed metros—sustaining high hardware utilization without compromising model convergence. Streaming DiLoCo is available today in MaxText v0.2.5 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Try it today for experimenting with distributed training workloads.
