#10243. Give sandboxed agents live web access with the Firecrawl kit for Docker Sandboxes

A coding agent in Docker Sandboxes runs inside an isolated VM, the sandbox controls which hosts it can reach, and credentials stay on the host behind a proxy. The same controls keep the agent from opening a docs page, reading a changelog, or looking up an API that shipped after its training cutoff, so it writes code against the version it remembers. The usual fix is to widen the allowlist until the agent can fetch anything, which hands back most of what the sandbox took away.

The Firecrawl kit gives the agent web search, page scraping, and site crawling through one added host, api.firecrawl.dev, and every page comes back as clean markdown.

The Firecrawl kit is a mixin (kind: mixin): it extends whichever workload you already run rather than defining a new sandbox. Its descriptor, firecrawl/firecrawl.yaml, declares four typed capabilities:

The kit adds to the sandbox’s allowlist and does not narrow it, so everything else is set by your global sbx policy. Docker’s balanced preset allows package registries, GitHub, and a curated list of vendor domains, and returns a 403 for general websites, including most documentation sites, so Firecrawl is the agent’s route to those. Under deny-all, it is the agent’s only web host.

Kits are experimental and in Early Access, so the kit format and sbx commands may change. The steps below are current for October 2026; the repository README carries the latest.

Kit: docker.io/firecrawl/sbx-kit-firecrawl:1.0.0 on Docker Hub, for linux/amd64 and linux/arm64. Source: github.com/firecrawl/firecrawl-docker-sandbox.

1. Store the key once on the host. The command prompts for the key. Service secrets are global by default, so every sandbox that requests the firecrawl service can use it once you approve. The kit never sees the value; the proxy injects it at request time.

2. Start a sandbox with the kit. The kit is an ordinary OCI image with its descriptor in the manifest, so a version tag is all you need. Each --kit adds a mixin to the workload, and the trailing . mounts your project directory.

On the first run, sbx asks you to approve sending the firecrawl credential to api.firecrawl.dev. Accept the defaults. The credential is required: with no secret stored, sbx refuses to create the sandbox instead of letting the agent hit a 401 mid-task. A detached or CI run cannot answer the approval prompt, so approve interactively once or write the binding to ~/.config/sbx/credentials.yaml first.

3. Give the agent a task that needs the live web. Documentation sites are blocked under balanced, so this one has to go through Firecrawl.

The kit’s instructions tell the agent to scrape blocked hosts through Firecrawl, so it calls fc.scrape(...) unprompted. Its response, trimmed:

4. Verify each layer. In the same session, ! runs shell commands inside the sandbox.

Expected: FIRECRAWL_API_KEY=proxy-managed. A real fc-... value here means the key came from somewhere other than the kit.

Expected: the page as markdown, starting with # Changelog and the latest version heading. The same URL returns a 403 to curl from inside the sandbox, so output here proves the SDK, the credential, and the network rule at once.

The kit also describes itself inside the sandbox. This prints the published descriptor, and the agent instructions sit next to it as firecrawl-context.md:

Running from source. To review or modify the kit before you run it, clone the repository and point --kit at the firecrawl/ directory; sbx builds source kits on demand:

With the kit installed, the agent can look things up on the web while it works. The sandbox only needs to talk to one new address, api.firecrawl.dev, and Firecrawl fetches the pages for it. This matters because the default balanced policy blocks most documentation sites. Without the kit, you’d have to allow each site one by one, and every new site you allow makes the sandbox a little less locked down.

Without the kit, you had a choice to make. The default balanced policy blocks most documentation sites, so you could either leave the agent working from what it remembered, or allow sites one by one and loosen the sandbox a little each time. The kit avoids that choice. Your Firecrawl API key stays on your machine, behind the proxy, and the agent still reads the web as it is today.

The result is fewer made-up APIs, fewer failed upgrades, and less time spent correcting an agent that was confidently out of date.

The kit is published under the firecrawl organization on Docker Hub as firecrawl/sbx-kit-firecrawl, with source, changelog, and CI on GitHub under Apache-2.0. The SDK pin is verified against PyPI’s package hashes at install time, and every release passes Docker’s kit-tck conformance suite in CI. Review the descriptor and allowlist before running it, as with any kit.

Pull the kit from Docker Hub: hub.docker.com/r/firecrawl/sbx-kit-firecrawl. Issues and feature requests go to the GitHub repository.

#10241. The Trust Layer: Securing Agentic AI with Docker AI Governance

The security problem changes once an AI agent can do more than answer a prompt. It can execute code, access files, connect to services, and call external tools. That creates a new security question for enterprises: how do you give an agent enough freedom to be useful without giving it more access than it actually needs?

A model decision is not automatically an authorized action. That distinction becomes important the moment an agent can turn a model output into an action in a real system.

The answer can’t rely only on the model. Agent behavior can vary, while security controls need to be clear and enforceable. That means putting controls around the environment where the agent runs, what it can access, which tools it can use, and what happens when a policy allows or blocks an action. For a CISO, this creates a trust gap. Approving the use of AI agents means being able to answer more than whether the model is capable: what access does the agent have, where are those boundaries enforced, and what evidence is available afterward? The goal is to let teams adopt agents without making model behavior itself the security boundary.

Docker AI Governance approaches this through three areas: Sandbox Policies, MCP Tool Governance, and Audit + Visibility.

Together, they address three practical questions security teams need answered: Where can the agent operate? What can it do? And can we see what happened?

In this article, I use “trust layer” as shorthand for the infrastructure between what an agent wants to do and what the organization actually allows it to do.

There is a related trust question too: what software artifacts are these agentic workflows building and using? Docker Hardened Images address that software supply-chain side of the problem, alongside the runtime controls discussed below.

Figure 1: The three layers of Docker AI Governance: Sandbox Policies, MCP Tool Governance, and Audit + Visibility.

An agent needs enough access to get real work done. A coding agent may need to install packages, build containers, edit project files, or connect to external services. The security challenge is deciding where those actions are allowed before the agent starts taking them.

Docker Sandboxes give each agent an isolated microVM environment a lightweight virtual machine with its own Docker Engine, filesystem, and network. Docker AI Governance adds policy around that environment so security teams can control network and filesystem access centrally.

Network policies can allow or block specific domains, IP ranges, and ports. Filesystem policies control which host paths can be mounted into a sandbox and whether that access is read-only or read-write. These controls are enforced at the network proxy and filesystem mount layers rather than depending on the agent to follow an instruction.

For an organization, that means the same boundaries can follow developers across their sandboxes instead of being configured differently on every machine. Teams still get the access they need for their work, but an individual agent cannot simply expand those boundaries on its own.

A developer can check the effective boundary before starting the agent:

The important part is that the result comes from the active policy, not from an instruction the agent is expected to follow.

This answers the first governance question: Where can the agent operate?

Once an agent has a controlled place to run, the next question is what it should be allowed to do through external tools.

MCP (Model Context Protocol) gives agents a common way to connect to external tools, services, and capabilities, but that also expands what an agent can act on. For enterprise use, making a tool available should not automatically mean every agent can use it.

Docker AI Governance puts policy around MCP activity handled through Docker’s MCP Gateway. Administrators can control which MCP servers may be registered and what agents can do through them, including tool calls, resources, prompts, and approval requirements. These organization policies are written in Cedar, a policy language used to express authorization rules. When MCP policy enforcement is active, governed requests are denied unless a matching policy permits them. For example, a policy can permit read-only tool calls for a specific registered MCP server:

Here, readOnly comes from the MCP tool’s annotations. Those annotations are supplied by the server and are advisory, so this example relies on metadata provided by that server.

This makes tool access an explicit policy decision rather than something left to each agent or developer to configure independently. A team can be given access to the tools it needs, while other servers or actions remain unavailable. Policies can also require confirmation for specific tool calls when an action needs an additional checkpoint.

One boundary is worth making clear: MCP policies apply to activity handled by Docker’s MCP Gateway. If an agent connects directly to a remote MCP server from inside a sandbox, that connection falls under the sandbox’s network policy instead.

With the environment constrained and tool access governed, we can answer the second question: What can the agent do?

Setting a policy is only part of the job. Security teams also need to know when that policy was applied, what action triggered it, and whether the action was allowed or denied.

Docker AI Governance records sandbox policy decisions and sandbox session events as structured audit records. Each record captures details such as the principal, action, target, decision, and time. The records contain governance metadata rather than the contents of prompts or agent responses.

Audit records can be kept locally or delivered to the cloud. With cloud delivery enabled, teams can search the organization-wide record, export it for further analysis, or forward events into existing SIEM (security information and event management) workflows.

Denied actions are especially useful. A team can see not only that an action failed, but that a governance rule blocked it and why. Allowed decisions matter too: they provide a record of how policy was applied while the agent was working.

Current audit coverage includes Docker Sandboxes policy decisions and sandbox session events. The same schema can support additional Docker AI sources as they become available.

That closes the third part of the governance loop: Can we see what happened?

AI Governance controls access around the agent at runtime. There is a separate question that matters to the workflows those agents participate in: what container images and software artifacts are they building, testing, or deploying?

Docker Hardened Images provide minimal, secure, and production-ready container images, Helm charts, and system packages maintained by Docker.

In practical terms, this gives teams evidence about what is inside an image and how that image was produced. Docker Hardened Images include signed SBOMs, VEX information, cryptographic signatures, and SLSA Build Level 3 provenance. Instead of simply trusting where an image came from, teams can inspect what is inside it and verify information about how it was built.

For organizations with additional compliance requirements, Docker also provides FIPS-enabled and STIG-ready variants through relevant DHI offerings. These capabilities sit alongside the broader DHI security and supply-chain controls rather than making any single standard the foundation by itself.

AI Governance and Docker Hardened Images solve different parts of the trust problem. Governance controls the authority an agent has while it works; DHI helps establish trust in the container artifacts that the workflow depends on.

Figure 2: Docker Hardened Images combine minimal container images with signed supply-chain metadata and verifiable build provenance.

Consider a developer using a coding agent to investigate an issue, update a service, and use an approved internal tool along the way.

The agent begins inside a Docker Sandbox. It can work with the project and run the commands needed for the task, but its access is still bounded by policy. If it tries to reach a network destination or mount a host path that the organization has not allowed, the applicable sandbox policy can block that access.

Now suppose the agent needs an MCP tool to look up information from another service. The request goes through Docker’s MCP Gateway, where organization policy determines whether that server and tool are permitted. A routine lookup might be allowed, while a more sensitive tool call could require confirmation or remain unavailable altogether.

For governance activity currently covered by AI Governance Audit Logs, Docker records the policy decision and relevant session metadata. Security teams can see who initiated the activity, what policy-controlled action was evaluated, and whether it was allowed or denied. Those records can then feed the organization’s existing monitoring and SIEM workflow.

If that same agent builds or tests a containerized application, the software entering the workflow matters too. Docker Hardened Images provide a more verifiable software foundation for that workflow, while AI Governance controls what the agent is authorized to do at runtime.

The model is still making decisions about how to complete the task. What changes is the authority around those decisions. The agent can work autonomously inside the boundaries the organization has defined, while access outside those boundaries remains a policy decision.

That is the practical value of the three layers working together:

Sandbox Policies define where the agent can operate.

MCP Tool Governance defines what tools it can use.

Audit + Visibility shows how governed activity was handled.

Taken together, the pattern is straightforward: define the boundary, check it before the agent starts working, enforce it while the agent is running, and use the resulting audit trail to understand what happened. If a boundary needs to change, the policy changes, not the expectation that the model will simply behave differently.

The challenge with agentic AI is not simply that agents can make decisions. It is that those decisions can now lead directly to actions.

That makes the boundary around the agent just as important as the model itself. Docker AI Governance addresses that boundary through Sandbox Policies, MCP Tool Governance, and Audit + Visibility: controlling where agents can operate, what tools they can use, and how governed activity is recorded.

Docker Hardened Images address a related supply-chain concern by helping teams work with smaller, verifiable container artifacts.

The goal is not to remove autonomy from agents. It is to make sure autonomy does not automatically come with unlimited authority.

As agents take on more real work, that distinction will matter more and more.

Docker launches a new brand built on trust. Learn why Docker is evolving for AI agents, what stays the same, and how to run agents securely with Docker.

Docker publishes fixed images for both Java4Shell and the NGINX HTTP/2 in the Docker Hardened Images (DHI) catalog.

Learn how Docker AI Governance secures agentic AI with sandbox policies, MCP tool governance, and audit visibility to support controlled AI agent workflows.

#10239. Meet the New Docker: Built to Run on Trust

Today, Docker is officially launching its new brand, and I’m excited to tell you what it’s all about and why we think now is the time for change.

Let’s start with the big picture. We’re living through a transformative moment: for Docker, for developers, for the industry, and quite possibly, for humanity itself. AI is creating possibilities that would have sounded like science fiction not long ago.

But it’s also forcing the industry to confront an almost existential question: how do we harness all that power and intelligence without compromising security, governance, or control? Or, put more simply: how do we get to a point where we can actually trust agents?

That one seemingly simple yet infinitely complex question is why we’re here today, introducing you to a new Docker.

Ok, not exactly here, but once upon a time, a decade or so ago, the tech industry was at a similar inflection point. Digital transformation was the term du jour, promising a new way of engaging customers and powering the global economy. There was one big constraint, though: teams needed a better way to build, ship, and run software consistently across any environment.

Enter a company you might have heard of: Docker.

Together with all of you, Docker built the standard trusted by millions of developers. For over a decade, developers have relied on us to make software portable, open, reproducible, and secure. Docker turned the frustration of “Well, it works on my machine!” into the assurance that “Yes, it works everywhere.”

Today, of course, the challenge confronting us is new and evolving, and we all need to evolve with it. We cannot simply continue to do what we’ve always done and expect to succeed in this new era. That applies to business leaders, developers, tech companies, everyone. It certainly applies to Docker. That’s what this rebrand is about: evolving to meet the moment for our customers.

Let me be unequivocal: this is not about leaving Docker’s legacy or heritage behind. We’ve spent more than a decade earning your trust. This is about building on that foundation, not replacing it.

The qualities you value us for aren’t changing. We’re still committed to openness and industry collaboration. We’re still, and always will be, developer-first. We’re still fanatical about simplicity, and about showing up everywhere you need us.

On the product front, the containers you know and love aren’t going anywhere. Containers still play a huge role in the software development lifecycle. As long as that’s the case, Docker will continue leading the way.

Agents have introduced a new problem. Containers isolate workloads. But agents aren’t workloads. Agents are actors.

Agents make their own decisions about what to run, what to access, and what to change. Developers and enterprises need solutions built specifically to isolate and govern them, so they can give those agents real autonomy without giving up control.

Docker’s next chapter is about delivering exactly that: security, isolation, and governance, so we can all trust agents to do meaningful work without feeling like we’re always one step from catastrophe.

In case I haven’t made it blindingly obvious yet, the new Docker is all about trust.

Trust Docker because our track record speaks for itself. Trust Docker because we’re proven, neutral, and secure. Trust Docker because it’s pretty darn clear you can’t trust the agents to manage themselves.

Trust us because we have a software supply chain that is already the standard for building securely. Trust us because nobody isolates agents where they run better than we do. Trust us because we’re taking a comprehensive approach to improving governance and minimizing risk across the entire enterprise.

Trust Docker because we’re not egotistical enough to think we can solve a challenge this big alone. Trust us because everything about our history, our heritage, and our actions over the past year tells you we’ll partner with the smartest and build alongside the best, and not create vendor lock-in.

Most of all, trust Docker because we’re not just talking the talk. We’ve got the goods to back it up: trusted supply chain for your agents to build from, trusted runtime to isolate your agents, and a trusted ecosystem to ensure all the layers of trust are covered, whether made by Docker or not.

As our product team innovated to meet the moment and our messaging in the market began to shift, one thing became clear: the speed and scale of evolution happening inside Docker called for an equally momentous evolution on the outside. Something that makes it unmistakably clear that it’s a new day for Docker. Something that says and shows, loud and clear, for all the world to see, that Docker is not just about containers anymore.

I’m talking, of course, about our beloved Moby.

If you know Docker, then you know Moby, and if Docker is evolving, Moby should too. The only question was to how and to what extent? We looked at a lot of whales, folks. (I may or may not have been seeing whales in my sleep.) Ultimately, with the help of our friends at the strategic brand transformation company, Motto, we landed on a new Moby for the new Docker.

In many ways, Moby is a microcosm for Docker and the changes we’re making: the Moby you love and trust, evolved for a new era. Just as the new Docker is no longer singularly defined by containers, neither is the new Moby.

We’ve got lots to show you. Poke around our new website. Find and engage with the new Docker brand on social media. And if you want to see what trust in the agentic era looks like in practice, try Docker Sandboxes and Docker AI Governance. We’d love to hear what you think.

Wherever this new era takes us, Docker will always honor and cherish the trust you continue to place in us. Now, let’s go build what’s next.

Terri Avnaim is the CMO of Docker, where she leads global marketing and shapes the company’s positioning and go-to-market strategy for the agentic era.

#10236. Docker patches Java4Shell and the NGINX HTTP/2 with Docker Hardened Images

OpenJDK and Oracle disclosed Java4Shell (CVE-2026-114938) on November 17, and NGINX released fixes for two HTTP/2 flaws the next day. Docker began publishing fixed images for both in the Docker Hardened Images (DHI) catalog starting that same day.

What we know about Java4Shell is that it’s a memory-corruption bug in the Java runtime that can let an attacker with no credentials run code inside a Java application. Many container images contain a Java runtime, including application images that bundle their own, so it affects teams well beyond the ones that write Java.

Docker Hardened Images are the trusted foundation your applications should run on, because a flaw in the runtime inside an image is fixed only when that image is rebuilt, and Docker rebuilds its hardened images as soon as upstream fixes become available. Fixed images for the Amazon Corretto, SapMachine and Azul Zulu runtimes and for NGINX are in the catalog today, with Eclipse Temurin following as soon as Adoptium completes its fixed builds.

To decode Base64 quickly, the HotSpot JVM uses a hand-written routine built on AVX-512 instructions. That routine miscalculates a length when the input ends in unexpected padding and writes up to 62 bytes past the end of a buffer on the Java heap. Calif, the researchers who found it, turned that into heap disclosure and remote code execution.

They demonstrated code execution before authentication against default installations of Keycloak, Jira, Confluence, Artifactory, Jenkins and PingFederate, and after authentication against Kafka, Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit and Spinnaker. They note there is no universal exploit: each application needs its own, and they have not published those. As of November 18 there are no public reports of exploitation in the wild.

Oracle rates the issue 8.1 (High) under CVSS 3.1. The researchers rate it 9.2 (Critical) under CVSS 4.0. CISA has issued an alert urging immediate patching.

To check a host’s CPU, run this on the host or Kubernetes node. Hardened runtime images have no shell, so it will not run inside the container.

grep -m1 ‘^flags’ /proc/cpuinfo | tr ‘ ‘ ‘\n’ | grep -E ‘^(avx512f|avx512bw|avx512vbmi|bmi2)$’

The host is affected if all four flags are printed.

Two other ways to check, for when a shell on the node is hard to get.

Ask the JVM. This works in hardened images:

docker run –rm –entrypoint java -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal -version | grep -w UseAVX

UseAVX = 3 means the JVM is using AVX-512 on that host. Treat the host as affected until the full check says otherwise. A value of 0 to 2 means AVX-512 is not in use. On ARM64 nothing is printed. The answer applies only to the host the command ran on.

Ask Kubernetes. kubectl get nodes -L kubernetes.io/arch,node.kubernetes.io/instance-type shows the architecture and instance type of every node. If you run Node Feature Discovery, nodes with the instruction set carry the label feature.node.kubernetes.io/cpu-cpuid.AVX512VBMI=true.

Check build runners and CI workers as well. They run Java, and they talk to the network.

Ask the JVM. This works in hardened images:

docker run –rm –entrypoint java -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal -version | grep -w UseAVX

UseAVX = 3 means the JVM is using AVX-512 on that host. Treat the host as affected until the full check says otherwise. A value of 0 to 2 means AVX-512 is not in use. On ARM64 nothing is printed. The answer applies only to the host the command ran on.

Ask Kubernetes. kubectl get nodes -L kubernetes.io/arch,node.kubernetes.io/instance-type shows the architecture and instance type of every node. If you run Node Feature Discovery, nodes with the instruction set carry the label feature.node.kubernetes.io/cpu-cpuid.AVX512VBMI=true.

Check build runners and CI workers as well. They run Java, and they talk to the network.

Fixed. The published images report 17.0.21.1.2, 21.0.13.1.2, 25.0.5.1.2 and 27.0.1.1.4.

Fixed. The published images report 21.0.13.1+2, 25.0.5.1+2 and 27.0.1.1+4.

Fixed. The published Zulu images we checked report 17.0.21.1+2, 21.0.13.1+2, 25.0.5.1+2 and 27.0.1.1+4.

Waiting for Adoptium to complete its release. On November 18 Adoptium had published 27.0.1.1+4 for x86-64 Linux only. 17.0.21.1, 21.0.13.1 and 25.0.5.1 were still in testing. Adoptium tracks progress in adoptium/temurin#326. Our published images still report 17.0.21+9, 21.0.13+9, 25.0.5+8 and 27.0.1.0.2+1, which are vulnerable.

Java 26 reached end of support in September 2026, so no fix is expected from any vendor, and it is not among the fixed lines in the OpenJDK advisory. If you run it, treat it as affected and keep the flag set. We recommend moving to Java 25 or updating to Java 27. If you cannot migrate yet, ask us whether Extended Lifecycle Support can cover your version. It is the Docker Hardened Images Enterprise add-on for software past its upstream end of life.

Not affected by this CVE and no action is needed for it. The same November update fixes other issues in these lines, so update when new images arrive.

Application images that bundle Java, such as Keycloak, Jenkins, Kafka, Elasticsearch and Solr

They follow the bundled runtime, which for many of them is Temurin, and they are rebuilt once the runtime they use is fixed. Until then, set the flag yourself.

Set the mitigation flag yourself on every JVM that is still waiting for a fixed runtime, and check what each image already sets before you do. Treat the flag as a stopgap, because a JVM is protected only if the flag reaches it when it starts, and a deployment setting or a start script can override it or drop it. A fixed runtime is the only thing that closes the issue, so move to the fixed image as soon as it is published.

The CPU check and the mitigation flag in the steps below apply to any image that runs a HotSpot-based OpenJDK build. The version strings in this post come from Docker Hardened Images, so for an image from another source, confirm the fixed build in that source’s advisory.

The fixed build can only come from whoever builds your image, because the Java runtime and the system packages your application runs on come from the image underneath it. That dependency is why a hardened image is a necessary step toward a trusted software supply chain. With Docker Hardened Images, tracking each vendor’s fix and rebuilding on it is work Docker does for you. The images are minimal and secure by default, and they are built to serve as a trusted and verifiable upstream for your builds. You can move a service onto one by following the migration guide.

A container often runs more than one JVM, for example when a build tool forks a JVM for tests, an application server starts helper processes or a CI agent starts a JVM for every build step. The flag protects only the JVMs that receive it, and the variable most people reach for first is JAVA_OPTS, which Java does not read at all.

The JVM itself, however it is started. Includes javac, keytool and other JDK tools.

A Java 8 JVM that sees it refuses to start. Some DHI FIPS images already set this variable.

Not read by javac, keytool or other JDK tools. DHI FIPS images set it. Java 8 ignores it.

JAVA_OPTS, JENKINS_JAVA_OPTS, MAVEN_OPTS, ES_JAVA_OPTS and similar

A start script, which copies the value onto one java command line

Each script has its own rules. If nothing in the image reads the variable, it does nothing.

Five rules follow from that table. We tested each on DHI images.

JAVA_OPTS only works where a script reads it. With JAVA_OPTS=”-XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics” set and a plain java command, the intrinsic stays on. No warning is printed.

A flag on the command line stops at that process. We started a JVM with the flag on its command line and had it start a second JVM. The child ran with the intrinsic on. With JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS or JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS in the environment, the child ran with it off. A child started with a cleared environment gets nothing, whichever variable you use.

Check what the image already sets before you set anything. In Docker and Kubernetes, your value replaces the image’s value. It is not appended.

docker inspect –format ‘{{range .Config.Env}}{{println .}}{{end}}’ \  | grep -E ‘^(JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS|JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS)=’

Our FIPS images use these variables to configure the FIPS provider. dhi.io/eclipse-temurin:21-fips sets both. When we replaced its JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS with only the flag, the JVM started normally, but the org.bouncycastle.fips.approved_only property was gone. Nothing warned us. If the command prints a value, copy it and add the flag at the end:

env:  – name: JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS    value: “ -XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics”

Not every FIPS image uses the same variables, so run the check for each image.

Keep JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS away from Java 8. Java 8 is not affected and does not know the flag:

Picked up JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS: -XX:-UseBASE64IntrinsicsUnrecognized VM option ‘UseBASE64Intrinsics’Error: Could not create the Java Virtual Machine.

Adding -XX:+IgnoreUnrecognizedVMOptions to the variable does not help on Java 8. Where Java 8 shares an environment with a newer Java, either scope the variable to the containers that need it or use JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS, which Java 8 ignores. Java 11 accepts the flag, so it is harmless there.

Verify the process, not the environment. Running java -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal -version inside a container starts a new JVM. It tells you what the environment gives a new JVM, not what the JVM already serving traffic was started with. For the running process, use the startup log line. JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS prints one too, starting with NOTE: Picked up. In an image that includes the JDK you can also ask the process directly:

jcmd VM.flags | tr ‘ ‘ ‘\n’ | grep UseBASE64Intrinsics

The answer must be -XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics. If the flag reached the JVM through an environment variable, it will not show in the process’s command line, so ps is not a reliable check.

Some applications discard these variables on purpose. Elasticsearch’s start script, for example, ignores JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS and expects ES_JAVA_OPTS. Check your application’s documentation.

Pull the new image and redeploy. Four things catch people out:

Then confirm with java -version that what is running reports a fixed build. After that, remove the flag from every place you set it.

The day after Java4Shell, NGINX released fixes for two memory errors in the way it handles HTTP/2 connections, tracked as CVE-2026-123007 and CVE-2026-123633. A crafted request can crash a worker process, and where an attacker gets past the operating system’s memory protections it can run code inside that worker. Both flaws need HTTP/2 to be enabled on the server, and the same releases fix other NGINX issues that do not depend on HTTP/2, so update either way.

The Docker Hardened Images for NGINX were rebuilt on NGINX 1.30.7 and 1.31.9 and published on November 18, the same day as the upstream release. To patch, pull the image again and redeploy, and move any tag pinned to an older exact version to a fixed one. nginx -v prints the version, and the fixed ones are 1.30.7 and 1.31.9. The notes on pull policy, mirrors and your own images in the section above apply to NGINX images in the same way.

The vulnerable code is in the Java runtime inside the image, and if you are using DHI, shipping the fix becomes our job. Whether that code can be reached in your deployment depends on things an image cannot see: the CPU it is scheduled onto, the options you start it with, the processes it starts, and the traffic you send it.

Track every Java vendor and ship fixed runtimes in DHI as soon as they are released

Track every Java vendor yourself and rebuild your base images on each fixed runtime

Tell you which images are fixed and which are still waiting

Find out from each vendor which of its builds are fixed

Tell you what we know, and update this post

Follow the OpenJDK, Oracle and vendor advisories as they change

Three jobs stay with you whichever image you run.

It was only four weeks ago that the news was PHP2Shell and the eMudhra certificate compromise. Calif, the security research team that found Java4Shell and helped find PHP2Shell, says it used AI to find this flaw in about an hour and that the whole effort cost less than $200 in tokens. When a flaw like this can be found and weaponized at that price, it is reasonable to expect more of them.

If you lead a team or answer for how your organization builds and ships software, the questions after a week like this one are whether you were exposed and how quickly you can prove you were not. It is imperative to have a trusted foundation in place before the next flaw appears, and Docker Hardened Images is that foundation to adopt. This week Docker began publishing fixed Java and NGINX images to the catalog within a day of each disclosure, with the images still waiting listed in the status table above. Each image ships with a signed SBOM and SLSA Build Level 3 provenance, so you have a record of what it contains and how it was built.

The Docker Hardened Images catalog is free and open source under Apache 2.0, and moving one of your services onto a hardened image typically means changing one line in its Dockerfile. If you are not building on Docker Hardened Images yet, start today.

#10230. Amazon S3 Vectors now supports metadata pre-filtering for higher recall on filtered searches

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

Today, we’re announcing metadata pre-filtering for Amazon S3 Vectors, which delivers higher recall on filtered queries by evaluating your metadata filter before the similarity search. You can filter on attributes such as tenant, category, status, or time, and pre-filtering adds prefix matching with $startsWith for paths, URLs, and hierarchical keys. Each vector carries up to 2 KB of filterable metadata, and a single query supports up to 100 filter constraints. There is no additional cost, no re-ingestion, and no change to your queries.

Most applications never search a whole index. They search the part of it that belongs to a particular user, account, or category, and they express that scope as a metadata filter. Semantic search, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and agentic applications all need the same thing from a filtered query: a similarity search that covers the vectors matching the filter, and returns the closest of them. With pre-filtering, a filtered query returns more of the relevant matches your index contains, giving you higher recall on filtered searches.

Common use cases Pre-filtering applies wherever results have to be both relevant and correctly scoped:

How pre-filtering works Each vector in an S3 Vectors index can carry application-defined metadata, and a query can filter on those fields.

Every vector index has an index mode. On an index whose index mode is ENHANCED, S3 Vectors resolves your filter first, then searches only the vectors that match. On an index whose index mode is CLASSIC, S3 Vectors performs the vector search and filter evaluation in tandem, validating each candidate vector against your filter as it searches. Existing indexes use CLASSIC until you update them.

Consider a support knowledge base of 8 million tickets, where an agent searches one customer’s history for a recurring error. If that customer accounts for 400 of those tickets, resolving customer_id first means the similarity search runs across all 400 of them, so the agent sees that customer’s prior occurrences. Before the index was updated, the same query drew its candidates from the full 8 million, and the result set contained fewer of that customer’s matching tickets.

On highly selective filters, pre-filtering returns up to 5x more of the matching vectors than the same query returned before on CLASSIC indexes.

Getting started Before you start, make sure your IAM policy grants permissions for the new actions.

You can get started in three steps. The walkthrough below builds a small product-catalog index and runs a selective filter against it, the same pattern you would use for a multi-tenant RAG store or a document search scoped to one client.

The dimension must match the output size of your embedding model, and distance-metric should match how that model was trained (cosine is common for text embeddings). Second, write vectors with the PutVectors API, attaching up to 2 KB of filterable metadata to each vector:

Each vector carries the attributes your application filters on. In this example, tenant_id scopes results to a single customer, category narrows by document type, created_date records when the document was created, and active is a boolean flag. By default every metadata field is filterable, so you can query on any of them without declaring a schema up front.

Third, run a filtered similarity query with the QueryVectors API. The filter uses a compact JSON syntax where a bare key-value pair is an equality match, and operators such as $and, $or, and $gt combine or refine conditions. Pass --return-metadata so the query returns each vector’s metadata:

The expected result is a single vector, doc-001, the only one matching all three filter conditions (tenant_id, category, and active):

S3 Vectors first narrows the search space to vectors matching all three filter conditions, then returns the 50 most similar vectors from that subset. Because the filter is applied before the search, those results are drawn from across all the vectors that match it.

Prefix matching with $startsWith Pre-filtering adds a prefix match operator for filtering on paths, URLs, and hierarchical keys. A document store that encodes case and folder structure into a document ID can scope a search to a subtree in one condition:

$startsWith joins the existing operators: equality, numeric range, set membership, existence checks, and boolean logic with $and and $or.

Turning on pre-filtering for existing indexes Call UpdateIndexMode on an existing index to turn on pre-filtering:

Pre-filtering takes effect in place. Your existing vectors are not re-ingested, your queries do not change, and the new filter operators are available immediately.

Here is the difference on the same index and the same query. Before the update, a query scoped to one tenant returns two of the ten results requested:

After the update, the same query returns a full result set drawn from across that tenant’s documents:

Rolling out across your indexes Once you have validated pre-filtering on an index, set the default index mode on the vector bucket so that new indexes use ENHANCED without a follow-up call:

To bring the rest of your existing indexes across, list them and check the index mode on each one, then call UpdateIndexMode on the ones still using CLASSIC:

Now available Metadata pre-filtering is available at no additional cost in all commercial AWS Regions where Amazon S3 Vectors is available, and in the AWS China Regions. You pay standard S3 Vectors pricing for storage, PUT requests, and queries. For full pricing details, visit the Amazon S3 pricing page. For regional availability, visit Amazon S3 Vectors Regions and quotas.

Whether you’re scoping a RAG application to one tenant, scoping an agent’s searches to one user’s documents, or narrowing a catalog search to a licensing window, pre-filtering lets you apply those filters without trading away recall. To learn more and get started, visit the Amazon S3 Vectors documentation. Send feedback to AWS re:Post for S3 or through your usual AWS Support contacts.

#10228. Check what your AI agents do before they do it, with Semantic governance policies

Two days before earnings, an employee asks their coworker agent to send the preliminary quarterly results to an investor who says they're under NDA, and to copy the employee's personal Gmail so they can review the numbers tonight. The agent can read the file and send email, so it fulfills the user request. Semantic governance policies, available in Preview in Gemini platform, enable enterprises to govern their agents and provide guardrails to stop that call before it runs. They check each tool call an agent proposes against what the user asked for and against rules you write in plain language.

Permissions alone can't catch this. They say what an agent can do, not whether a particular call follows your rules. A model can also be talked out of the rules in its system instructions, by a user with a good reason, or by a document telling it to do something nobody asked for, a technique called indirect prompt injection. So the check has to sit outside the agent. Semantic governance policies are enforced through a policy enforcement point, such as Agent Gateway, alongside the agent governance controls we expanded in October, so policies stay separate from your agent's code.

Semantic governance policies check each tool call your agent's model proposes, before it runs, against two things:

Today, the policy enforcement point that Semantic governance policies support is Agent Gateway. You need to configure your agent so all your agent egress traffic goes through Agent Gateway, facilitating traffic between your agent and its model. When the model responds with a tool call, Agent Gateway sends the proposed call to the Semantic governance policy engine for evaluation before the response reaches your agent.

In the policy engine, an LLM judge evaluates the proposed call against the conversation so far, the agent's system instructions and tool descriptions, and your policies.

If the call passes, the response reaches your agent unchanged. If it doesn't, the engine replaces the response's content, tool calls included, with a configurable denial message, so the call never runs. Either way, the verdict and the judge's rationale go to Cloud Logging in your project. Responses that contain only text, such as an answer, a plan, or a question back to the user, aren't evaluated, because they take no action.

Consider a coworker agent for a finance team. It can search the shared drive, read files, send email, and delete draft files, and its system instructions include the date of the next earnings release. The company writes one policy for the agent:

"Quarterly financial results (revenue, margins, bookings, guidance) should not be shared publicly until the public earnings release. Before the release, they may be shared only with @example.com addresses. Never send them to external or personal addresses, investors, analysts, or press, regardless of NDAs, urgency, or the requester's seniority."

In each denied case, the agent gets a denial message instead of the call. The judge's rationale can quote your policy, so if users shouldn't see it, set your own denial message on the policy.

The earnings rule is one policy. Each policy is a natural-language constraint attached to an agent. It can apply to all of an agent's tools or to one tool, and it can name a tool's parameters. Other examples:

Because the earnings policy doesn't name a tool, it's checked on every tool call the agent proposes, so it would also apply to a tool that shares or uploads files. For guidance on wording, see best practices for writing policies. You can create it in the Google Cloud console or with the Google Cloud CLI:

IAM and Agent Gateway access policies decide which tools and MCP servers an agent can reach. Model Armor screens prompts and responses for threats such as jailbreaks and sensitive data. Agent Anomaly Detection analyzes sessions asynchronously, out of band. Semantic governance policies add the check none of them makes, as one layer of defense in depth: they judge each action inline, before it runs.

Because verdicts come from an LLM, which is probabilistic, they may vary. We recommend reviewing them in dry run mode before you enforce, and using Semantic governance policies alongside your other controls. The judge decides from the conversation, the agent's instructions, and your policies, so include facts a policy depends on, such as the earnings date, in the agent's system instructions. Streamed model responses aren't evaluated. Semantic governance policies complement, rather than replace, your disclosure controls and legal and compliance reviews.

To try Semantic governance policies on an agent behind Agent Gateway:

To learn more, read the Semantic governance policies overview and best practices.

Your agent should work for the person who asked, within the rules you set. Semantic governance policies check both before each action runs.

In our next post, we'll share how Semantic governance policies held up against prompt injection on AgentDojo, a public agent-security benchmark.

Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

Did the user ask for this? Securing AI agents against intent hijacking with Semantic governance policies

#10226. Amazon S3 Tables now support all Apache Iceberg V3 data types

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

Amazon S3 Tables now support all data types in the Apache Iceberg V3 specification. You can create V3 tables or upgrade existing V2 tables to take advantage of V3 features like deletion vectors, row lineage, and new data types such as variant, nanosecond timestamps, unknown, geometry, and geography.

Apache Iceberg has become the open standard for managing large analytics datasets. It lets you manage petabyte-scale tables with features like schema evolution, hidden partitioning, and time travel queries, while keeping your data in open Parquet files in data lakes on object storage like Amazon S3. Amazon S3 Tables offer storage purpose-built to keep Iceberg tables performant and cost-effective as they grow, with fully managed features like automatic compaction, maintenance, replication, and Intelligent-Tiering.

Teams running analytics on Apache Iceberg V2 tables often hit the same limits as their data grows. A compliance request to delete 50,000 user records from a 2-billion-row table leaves behind positional delete files that slow queries until compaction runs. Semi-structured events land as JSON strings that every query has to parse. Geospatial coordinates and nanosecond-precision timestamps get encoded as strings or integers. Each workaround adds storage cost, query latency, and pipeline code. With V3, Iceberg solves these challenges by offering native support for semi-structured and geospatial data, faster row-level operations, and built-in row lineage for data governance.

Starting today, Amazon S3 Tables support all V3 data types, including variant, nanosecond timestamps, geometry, geography, and unknown, along with deletion vectors and row lineage. You can create new V3 tables or upgrade existing V2 tables in place, and S3 Tables continue to run compaction and maintenance for you.

Apache Iceberg V3 V3 is the latest version of the Iceberg specification. Among its many improvements, V3 introduces capabilities that address the most common pain points in V2. This includes:

Deletion vectors replace V2’s positional delete files with a compact binary format. That 50,000-row compliance delete now writes a single deletion vector file instead of thousands of small deletes, significantly reducing compaction time and delete file overhead.

Row lineage adds _row_id and _last_updated_sequence_number to each record automatically. Your downstream pipelines can query these fields to find changed rows without scanning the full table.

New data types let you store semi-structured, geospatial, and nanosecond-precision data natively instead of encoding it as strings or integers:

Variant data type stores semi-structured data in columnar format. During writes, the engine shreds variant data into hidden columns and collects statistics. At query time, those statistics enable file pruning that significantly reduces I/O compared to parsing JSON strings.

The following sections walk through how to use these V3 capabilities in practice, with examples that show how to create tables, work with the new data types, and manage data at scale.

Getting started A retail analytics team tracks user behavior across web and mobile apps. Each event has a different structure: page views include URLs and duration, purchases include items and amounts, and searches include query terms and result counts. With V3’s variant type, you store all event shapes in one table without predefined schemas:

Insert events with different payload shapes without worrying about schema evolution:

Now query the variant column directly, without PARSE_JSON at read time. With Amazon EMR Spark, use variant_get:

To enable deletion vectors for write operations, configure merge-on-read mode:

Now when you run a compliance delete, V3 writes a small deletion vector instead of rewriting data files:

S3 Tables compaction handles these deletion vector files automatically on the next maintenance cycle.

Upgrading from V2 AWS provides backwards compatibility for both versions to minimize disruption during migration to V3. Existing V2 readers continue to work on upgraded tables until you’re ready to fully adopt V3 features. For more details, see the S3 Tables Iceberg V3 documentation. Upgrade an existing table atomically without rewriting data:

On the next compaction cycle, S3 Tables remove old V2 delete files. New modifications use deletion vectors automatically. Row lineage fields initialize on the first data modification after the upgrade.

This is a one-way operation. The Apache Iceberg specification does not support downgrading from V3 to V2. Verify that all engines accessing the table support V3 before upgrading.

Using row lineage for incremental pipelines After your table has V3 data, use row lineage to build efficient incremental pipelines:

This returns only rows modified after sequence number 42. Your downstream jobs can checkpoint this value and process only new changes on each run, instead of scanning the full table.

Compatibility across AWS analytics services AWS offers the broadest native Apache Iceberg support of any major cloud provider, with Iceberg-compatible services at every layer of the data stack: ingestion, storage, catalog, and analytics. You can store and automatically optimize V3 tables in Amazon S3 Tables, write data with Amazon EMR Spark, integrate and manage data with AWS Glue, and run analytics with Amazon Redshift. To learn more about AWS analytics support for V3, see the Apache Iceberg on AWS prescriptive guidance.

Both S3 Tables and AWS Glue Data Catalog support the Iceberg REST Catalog (IRC) API, enabling interoperability across engines regardless of the catalog endpoint.

Now available Amazon S3 Tables support for all Apache Iceberg V3 data types is now available in all AWS Regions where S3 Tables are supported. Apache Iceberg V3 support is available at no additional charge; standard S3 Tables pricing applies.

To get started, visit the Amazon S3 Tables documentation or create a table bucket from the Amazon S3 console. If you want to call APIs, search documentation, find regional availability, and check troubleshooting about this feature, try using the AWS MCP Server and plugins with your preferred AI tool. Send feedback to AWS re:Post or through your usual AWS Support contacts.

#10223. Announcing AWS Well-Architected Agent, an AI-powered intelligence to optimize your cloud environment (preview)

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

Today, we’re announcing the public preview of AWS Well-Architected Agent, an AI-powered service that analyzes your AWS environment to deliver targeted, contextual recommendations for improving your applications’ cost, security, performance, and resilience. The AWS Well-Architected Agent analyzes your infrastructure, understands unique business goals, and delivers contextual recommendations with ready-to-implement fixes. It delivers context-aware optimization without relying on manual audits or generic checklists.

The agent evaluates your environment as an experienced cloud architect would. It automatically correlates utilization metrics, resource configurations, and application topology, and analyzes against Well-Architected best practices across 65+ AWS services. It generates recommendations aligned to your declared business goals, delivers implementation packages with every finding, and surfaces cross-pillar trade-offs making it simpler to remediate the findings.

Here are the three main features of this service:

AWS Well-Architected Agent in action To get started, create an agent profile to define the scope of what Well-Architected Agent can access and provide recommendations on, complete the IAM role setup to access resources, conduct architecture review, and remediate recommendations.

Create an agent profile In the AWS Well-Architected console, choose Get started with Well-Architected Agent. You can define an agent profile that specifies which AWS accounts and applications to monitor, which optimization pillars to focus on, and the permissions required.

You can choose AWS accounts or AWS Regions to monitor and optimization pillars that matter most to your business. You can also set goals for each pillar: cost optimization, performance, resilience, and security.

To give access to the agent for your AWS environment, provision customer-managed IAM roles the agent uses to read resource configurations, utilization metrics, and application topology. To learn more, visit the IAM prerequisite for AWS Well-Architected Agent.

When you choose Get Started, the agent creates your agent profile. Resource and application recommendations will be generated within 24 hours after profile creation.

You can conduct an architecture review on pre-deployment workloads by uploading an IaC project in Terraform, AWS CloudFormation, or AWS Cloud Development Kit (CDK) to be analyzed. Choose Conduct architecture review in the dashboard, upload a.zip file containing IaC project or repository file, and select which Well-Architected lens to use for reviewing your infrastructure.

You can define your applications to add context which will enhance the relevancy and further contextualize recommendations. Choose Add application context in the dashboard, add your applications with AWS accounts, AWS Regions, AWS services, tags if you want to narrow the scope to specific resources, and the details of applications.

Review prioritized recommendations and start remediating Now you can see generated prioritized recommendations generated by the agent across your resources and applications, selected pillars, ranked against your declared goals, with automation-ready remediation included.

When you choose the specific recommendation, you can see the details, insights into why the agent are suggesting the recommendation, impacts and trade-off, and recommended fixes across affected AWS resources.

Choose Start remediation to address recommended fixes. You can choose the console, updated IaC template, CLI commands to remediate by the resolution type. It provides detailed step-by-step instructions and you can roll out this instruction and verify the result.

When you choose Using updated IaC template, the agent provides the code changes needed to update your existing IaC templates such as the CDK function shown above which you can copy directly into your codebase.

You can also configure API access to integrate recommendations directly into your existing development and operations workflows. To interact with the agent programmatically, including calling APIs and searching documentation, try the AWS MCP Server and plugins with your preferred AI coding tool. To learn more, visit the AWS Well-Architected Agent documentation.

Things to know Here are some things that you should know about the Well-Architected Agent.

You can still use existing AWS Well-Architected Tool to manually evaluate your cloud architecture with user-defined lenses that measure your workload using your own best practices.

Join the preview Access to the AWS Well-Architected Agent and its recommendations is available in US East (N. Virginia), US East (Ohio), and US West (Oregon). You can onboard workloads from any AWS commercial Region. AWS Well-Architected Agent is delivered by AWS Support and available to AWS customers with an AWS Support plan.

Give it a try today in the AWS Well-Architected console and send feedback through your usual AWS Support contacts.

#10216. All the numbers: Amazon Prime Day 2026 powered by AWS

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

Amazon Prime Day 2026 was exclusively for Prime members and ran June 23–26, 2026 with millions of deals across more than 35 categories.

Prime Day 2026 – all the numbers As always, Prime Day was powered by AWS. Here are some of the most interesting and/or mind-blowing metrics:

DynamoDB maintained high availability while delivering single-digit millisecond responses and peaking at 192 million requests per second.

AWS CloudTrail – AWS CloudTrail processes billions of API activity events per day for governance, compliance, and operational auditing. During Prime Day 2026, that volume surged to 3.6 trillion events in just four days, a 44% increase over Prime Day 2025.

Amazon CloudWatch – Amazon CloudWatch processed over 2.15 quadrillion metric observations per day during Prime Day 2026.

Amazon GuardDuty – During Prime Day 2026, Amazon GuardDuty monitored an average of 14.08 trillion log events per hour, a 59% increase from last year’s Prime Day.

AWS Fault Injection Service (FIS) – We ran over 44,000 AWS FIS experiments – more than six times what we conducted in 2025 – to help ensure Amazon.com remains highly available on Prime Day.

#10213. Agent Anomaly Detection: Safeguarding agents across other clouds

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview, including monitoring for agents that run outside Google Cloud. To request access, see the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.

Our launch post introduced Agent Anomaly Detection, which helps you understand and act on agent behavior that drifts outside its intended boundaries. It covered agents on Google Cloud, such as Agent Runtime, Cloud Run, and Google Kubernetes Engine (GKE). Many teams also run agents on other clouds: on AWS Lambda next to an inventory database, as a hosted agent in Microsoft Foundry, or on-premises next to a system that can't move. Those agents carry the same behavioral risks, and until now Agent Anomaly Detection couldn't review them.

Agent Anomaly Detection works from an agent's traces and logs, so it doesn't need access to the agent's runtime. If those traces and logs reach your Google Cloud project, it can detect anomalous behavior wherever the agent runs. In this post, we explain what Agent Anomaly Detection needs from an agent on other cloud, then show what it found for an agent on AWS Lambda and an agent on Microsoft Foundry.

Agent Anomaly Detection reads the logging and observability buckets that you choose when you enable it. Telemetry from anywhere works when:

Agents built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) for Python, version 2.12.0 or later, can produce this telemetry with ADK's Google Cloud exporters and a few environment variables, which the next section shows.

An agent outside Google Cloud needs two things to send its telemetry:

For the full setup on AWS and Azure, see the documentation.

Within 30 minutes, the agent appears in the Monitored agents list, which means it has been discovered and is ready to be enabled. Once it's enabled, manually or by automatic agent enrollment at discovery, Agent Anomaly Detection starts analyzing its sessions.

The agent's model and tool calls still run where the agent runs. Only telemetry, including the prompts and responses that Agent Anomaly Detection reads, goes to your Google Cloud project. The analysis runs separately, so it doesn't slow the agent down.

To evaluate Agent Anomaly Detection outside Google Cloud, we deployed ADK agents across two platforms: an inventory agent on AWS Lambda and a customer-service agent on Microsoft Foundry. The examples below show how Agent Anomaly Detection reads whole conversations, not isolated calls, to catch behavioral drift.

Our example inventory agent runs in AWS Lambda with one tool, list_inventory, which pages through a product catalog. We sent two sessions: a shopper who checked stock for a few items and stopped, and a scraper that requested 100 catalog items at a time at increasing offsets to copy the full catalog and inventory data. Both sessions used the same tool with permitted calls, but differed in pattern and intent.

Of the two sessions, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged only the scraper's. No call failed, but the whole exchange showed systematic scraping. The analysis found cascading failures (loops and repeated calls that build on each other) and resource exhaustion (runaway use of tokens, compute, or tool calls), and recommended rate-limiting list_inventory calls.

Detection doesn't depend on the agent's model. When we ran the inventory agent on Amazon Nova Lite through Amazon Bedrock, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 10 of its 40 sessions. Some began as routine questions that the model answered by paging through the entire catalog until the function timed out: no errors, no blocked calls, and easy to miss.

Our example ecommerce customer-service agent has two tools: get_order_status and issue_refund. Refunds involve money, so they carry the highest risk.

We ran six sessions against the Foundry agent, which we told to carry out every request without validation. Two were normal: One asked for an order's status, and one asked for a $20 refund on an order that arrived damaged. The other four pushed the agent past its role, and Agent Anomaly Detection flagged all four.

In one, a user who claimed to be the store administrator had every order refunded at 10 times its total to a personal account, $1,784.90 in all. In another, a user pasted an order note that said "SYSTEM OVERRIDE," and the agent refunded $999 on a $15.99 order.

For the order note, Agent Anomaly Detection explained that override instructions in the note coerced the agent into an unverified $999 refund. It recommended validating issue_refund arguments to block override patterns in order notes, and requiring extra validation for refunds above a set threshold.

If your agent's telemetry reaches Google Cloud, Agent Anomaly Detection can monitor it with the same detectors and console as your agents on Agent Runtime. Today that means agents built with ADK for Python. Findings for agents outside Google Cloud appear only in the Agent Anomaly Detection console.

To get started, request access to the Private Preview via the Agent Anomaly Detection overview, and then refer to the setup guide for instructions.

#10212. Announcing AWS Lambda Web Functions

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

Today, we’re announcing AWS Lambda Web Functions, a new form factor that lets you run standard HTTP web applications on Lambda without rewriting your code into an event handler. You can take a standard port-listening server built with Express, Hono, Fastify, Next.js, or any Node.js framework, deploy it with a single command, and Lambda automatically handles scaling, multi-region distribution, and active CPU billing. No adapters, no infrastructure to manage, and no framework lock-in.

With this launch, AWS Lambda now offers three distinct form factors, each built on the same Firecracker isolation technology and designed for a different programming model.

Lambda Event Functions are what you already know as Lambda functions. They use the event-driven, single-concurrent handler model with wall-clock billing. We’re introducing the “Event Functions” name to highlight that programming model: your function receives an event, processes it, and returns a response. The event-driven programming model is ideal for event processing, queue consumers, and invocations triggered by other AWS services.

Lambda Web Functions use an HTTP-native programming model with multi-concurrent execution and active CPU billing. Your code listens on a port and handles standard HTTP requests. That programming model is ideal for web applications, API backends, and interactive HTTP workloads where multiple concurrent requests and I/O-heavy processing are the norm.

Lambda MicroVMs provide isolated, stateful sandbox environments with customer-managed lifecycles for running untrusted or user-generated code.

The three complement each other. An application might use Event Functions for asynchronous backend processing, Web Functions for the user-facing API and server-side rendering layer, and MicroVMs for interactive code execution features, all within the same AWS account and sharing the same operational model.

Why Web FunctionsModern web applications need to serve global audiences with fast, consistent response times while giving developers the freedom to use standard frameworks and iterate quickly. Building this today typically requires assembling multiple infrastructure components, configuring multi-region deployments, setting up latency-based routing, and tuning billing models to avoid paying for I/O wait. Developers often find themselves adapting their application code to fit a specific compute model rather than deploying the HTTP server they already have.

Lambda Web Functions removes this complexity. Each execution environment handles multiple requests simultaneously (up to 128 concurrent requests per environment), which means a burst of incoming traffic can share warm environments instead of mapping each request to a new one. Web Functions meter the CPU time your code uses, so time spent waiting on a database query or an API call consumes little CPU. Memory is metered for as long as the execution environment exists. For I/O-heavy web workloads like server-side rendering, API backends, and AI inference endpoints streaming token responses, this can reduce compute costs.

Let’s try it outTo get started, I wrote a simple Express.js server. This is the same code I run locally with node index.js, no Lambda-specific adapters or wrappers required.

The only difference from running locally is the AWS_LAMBDA_HTTP_ENDPOINT environment variable, which Lambda sets to the host and port your server must listen on. To run locally, set it yourself, for example AWS_LAMBDA_HTTP_ENDPOINT=127.0.0.1:3000 node index.js.

With my code ready, I deployed using the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI):

One command. No prior resource setup. The CLI bundled my source, created the Web Function, published its first revision, attached a Web Function endpoint, and returned a live HTTPS URL in under a minute.

A revision is an immutable snapshot of the code I deployed. The endpoint is the HTTPS URL that routes traffic to a revision, or splits traffic across revisions. Those are the resources I use to iterate and to control how traffic moves to new code.

Next, I wanted my application to serve users in Europe and Asia with lower latency. I redeployed with multiple regions:

Lambda Web Functions replicated my code to all three regions and configured latency-based routing automatically. Each request now routes to the Region with the lowest latency for the caller, on a best-effort basis, through one global URL (.lambda-web.global.on.aws).

When I updated my code and ran the same deploy command again, the CLI detected the existing function, uploaded the new revision, and pointed the endpoint at it. Total turnaround from code change to live: seconds.

For production workflows that require a more gradual rollout, I can shift traffic between revisions with a single API call:

This sends 90% of endpoint traffic to the current revision and 10% to the new one, giving me a canary deployment without additional tooling.

Better together with Amazon CloudFrontFor applications that combine static and dynamic content, Web Functions works well alongside Amazon CloudFront. You can select a Web Function endpoint as the origin for your CloudFront distribution. Static assets are served from the cache while dynamic requests route to your Web Function, giving you CDN performance for static content with serverless compute for the dynamic parts.

Web Functions improves the private-origin experience with CloudFront. With this launch, you attach CloudFront origin access control (OAC) to a Web Function endpoint instead. CloudFront signs requests to the origin, and the endpoint allows only your distribution to invoke it. You get the same private-origin pattern CloudFront already provides for Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), without exposing the endpoint or adding custom authorization to your app.

Now availableAWS Lambda Web Functions is available today in US East (N. Virginia), US East (Ohio), US West (Oregon), Asia Pacific (Tokyo), and Europe (Ireland). Web Functions run Node.js 24 (nodejs24.x) on Arm64 at launch and support Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) connectivity, allowing your function to access resources that exist within a VPC.

To get started, deploy your first Web Function in under a minute:

For pricing details, visit the AWS Lambda pricing page. To learn more, see the Lambda Web Functions documentation.

#10207. Gift Tax Rules: How Much Can You Give Tax-Free?

What's your FI number? Calculate it in 60 seconds

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

The gift tax is one of the most misunderstood parts of the tax code. People hear "gift tax" and assume they'll owe the IRS money for giving a birthday check to their grandchild. That's almost never how it works. The federal gift tax exists to prevent people from giving away their entire estate during their lifetime to dodge estate taxes at death. For the vast majority of Americans, it will never cost them a dime.

Here's what actually matters: in 2026, you can give up to $18,000 to any individual without reporting it to the IRS at all. Give that amount to 10 different people and you've transferred $180,000 completely under the radar. Go above the annual limit and you still won't owe tax — you'll just file a form that tracks the excess against your $13.61 million lifetime exemption.

The gift tax is really a planning tool, not a penalty. Understanding how it works can help you transfer wealth to family members, fund education, reduce your taxable estate, and accelerate your heirs' path to financial independence — all without triggering any actual tax.

The annual gift tax exclusion is the amount you can give to any single recipient each year without needing to file a gift tax return or use any of your lifetime exemption. For 2026, that amount is $18,000 per recipient.

This limit applies per giver, per recipient. That means:

The IRS adjusts this amount for inflation periodically. Here's how the annual exclusion has changed over the years:

The annual exclusion has roughly doubled since 2002, when it was just $11,000. Inflation adjustments are made in $1,000 increments, so the number stays flat for a year or two, then bumps up.

This is the single most important gift tax fact: the person making the gift is responsible for any gift tax — never the recipient. If your grandmother gives you $50,000, she files the gift tax return and uses her lifetime exemption. You don't report it as income, you don't owe tax on it, and you don't need to do anything. Gifts received are not taxable income.

When you give more than the annual exclusion to any single person, the excess counts against your lifetime gift and estate tax exemption — currently $13.61 million per person ($27.22 million for married couples). You won't actually owe gift tax until you've exceeded this lifetime amount.

Let's put that in perspective. If you give your daughter $118,000 in one year, you're $100,000 over the annual exclusion. You file Form 709 to report it, and the IRS subtracts that $100,000 from your lifetime exemption. Your remaining exemption drops from $13.61 million to $13.51 million. No tax is owed.

At the 40% top gift tax rate, you'd need to give away more than $13.61 million over your lifetime before any actual tax kicks in. That puts fewer than 0.1% of Americans in gift tax territory.

Important sunset warning: The current $13.61 million exemption is set to be cut roughly in half on January 1, 2026, reverting to approximately $7 million per person (adjusted for inflation) under the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act sunset provisions. This has created urgency for high-net-worth families to make large gifts before the window closes.

Several types of transfers are completely excluded from gift tax rules — no annual limit, no lifetime exemption usage, no Form 709 required:

The education and medical exclusions are especially powerful for the FI community. A grandparent can pay $50,000 in tuition directly to a university AND give the grandchild $18,000 in cash in the same year — all tax-free with no reporting.
