#10174. AWS Weekly Roundup: AWS Nitro Sandboxes, AWS Emulator, and more (November 16, 2026)

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

A second delivery of the same message can reveal more about an application than its happy-path demo. I like tools that make those cases easier to investigate while a change is still small. Last week’s launch of AWS Emulator brings part of that work onto your own machine: developers and coding agents can exercise AWS API interactions, inspect results, and try again without provisioning cloud resources for every attempt.

I wrote about the Emulator’s launch last week, and it’s my main highlight in this edition. There are also new ways to isolate workloads on shared infrastructure, give an agent narrowly scoped access to a backend, and prepare search applications for a regional disruption. Now, let’s get into this week’s AWS news…

AWS Emulator runs locally alongside your code and development tools. Image: AWS.

HeadlinesTest your applications against AWS locally with AWS Emulator introduces a generally available local environment for AWS API interactions. Point the AWS CLI or AWS SDKs at its HTTPS endpoint, and your application keeps its normal SDK calls. The walkthrough shows a coding agent creating an Amazon DynamoDB table, writing an item, and reading it back. You don’t need an AWS account for emulated operations, and there is no additional service fee. The getting-started guide covers Linux packages, Docker for macOS and Windows, and agent-assisted setup; the source is on GitHub.

What I like is giving an agent concrete feedback while it works: run calls, inspect results, and revise the code. Matt Wood’s perspective on feedback for coding agents explores why that loop matters. Check the published fidelity results for your operations, because coverage varies and unimplemented calls return HTTP 501. The emulator doesn’t reproduce IAM and resource-policy enforcement or cross-account and cross-Region behavior, so test the complete application and its security configuration on AWS before production.

AWS Nitro Sandboxes bring hardware isolation to pods, tasks, and VMs. You can give an Amazon EKS pod or Amazon ECS task its own VM boundary while sharing an Amazon EC2 instance. That is useful for tenant workloads, build jobs, and agents executing generated code. Isolated mode separates workloads from their neighbors; confidential mode adds protection from node operators and cryptographic attestation. The Compute Blog deep dive explains both modes and their integrations. Nitro Sandboxes have no additional charge on supported instances; EC2 and applicable EKS Auto Mode or ECS Managed Instances fees still apply.

Last week’s launches Here are some launches and updates from this past week that caught my attention:

Amazon Cognito supports OAuth 2.0 token exchange – You can now exchange a user’s token for one scoped to a downstream service in Amazon Cognito. The derived token can have narrower permissions and a single audience, with no refresh token; supplying an actor token also identifies the intermediary in an act claim. For an agent calling a backend on someone’s behalf, that makes delegation more explicit without forwarding the original token. The feature is available on Essentials and Plus plans in all Cognito Regions.

Claude Fable 5.2 is available on AWS – You can access the model through Amazon Bedrock or Claude Platform on AWS. Anthropic positions it as its most capable generally available model for technical work, including extended engineering tasks, scientific research, and quantitative finance. Bedrock has model-specific retention requirements: Fable 5.2 traffic is retained for up to 30 days for abuse detection, with a temporary zero-retention exception for eligible internal use.

Amazon ElastiCache Serverless adds search and aggregations – You can now combine vector, full-text, and hybrid search with server-side aggregations in Amazon ElastiCache Serverless for Valkey. That gives product discovery, recommendations, and agent-memory retrieval another option for querying frequently changing data. The capability requires Valkey 9, so create a compatible cache or upgrade an existing one. It is available in commercial Regions, AWS GovCloud (US), and the AWS European Sovereign Cloud.

Amazon OpenSearch Service adds managed cross-Region disaster recovery – You can now pair Amazon OpenSearch Service domains running version 3.7 or later in two Regions within one account. AWS specifies recovery objectives under one minute for data loss and under 20 minutes for recovery time. Planned switchovers preserve all writes; unplanned failovers can lose unreplicated data. The deep dive covers the remaining work: keeping configuration aligned and redirecting application writes after switching roles, plus secondary-domain and data-transfer charges.

Amazon CloudWatch Logs adds account-level retention policies – You can now set retention across all log groups or groups matching a name prefix in Amazon CloudWatch, with policies covering existing groups or only new ones. When policies overlap, the longest retention period applies. A platform team can set a baseline and retain security logs longer without updating every group individually. The feature has no additional charge and is available in commercial Regions except Middle East (Bahrain) and Middle East (UAE).

The next generation AgentCore runtime is now the default – New Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtimes now use the next-generation runtime by default, so agent sessions start in under 2 seconds, scale up to 8 vCPU and 32 GB, scale to zero when idle, and bill up to 68% less memory, while each session still runs in its own isolated microVM.

For a full list of AWS announcements, be sure to keep an eye on the What’s New with AWS page.

Other AWS news Here are some additional posts and resources that you might find interesting:

Optimizing cost per task and proving agent ROI on Amazon Bedrock – This guide follows an agent’s cost through repeated calls, reasoning, caching, and human review. I like its emphasis on measuring a completed task and comparing outcomes against a documented baseline. Its worked prices are illustrative; use your own workload and reconcile estimated costs with billing data.

How Zillow runs 100 million daily notifications on AWS Step Functions – Zillow describes reusable content modules and configuration-driven AWS Step Functions Express Workflows for more than 100 million daily notifications. The useful engineering details include idempotent modules, batching, campaign isolation, and testing expressions with the TestState API before deployment. Those patterns apply well beyond notification systems.

#10168. How to Start a Side Business While Working Full-Time

Connect with people pursuing financial independence, get practical advice, and find local groups on the path to freedom.

What's your FI number? Calculate it in 60 seconds

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

The number one reason side businesses fail is not lack of funding, bad timing, or tough competition — it is building something nobody wants to buy. Before you spend a dollar on a website, business cards, or inventory, you need to validate that real people will pay real money for what you are offering.

Validation does not require a perfect product. It requires proof of demand. Talk to 10 people who match your target customer. Describe what you plan to offer. Ask two questions: "Would you pay for this?" and "How much would you pay?" If 3 or more say yes with a specific dollar amount, you have a viable idea. If most say "that sounds cool" but hesitate on paying, you have a hobby.

The FI community has a particular advantage here. Many FI seekers have already cut their expenses to the point where a side business does not need to generate massive revenue to make a meaningful impact. Even $500 to $1,000 per month in side business income — invested consistently — can shave years off your FI timeline.

Forget building a $1 million business. Your only goal is reaching $1,000 in total revenue as quickly as possible. That first $1,000 proves three things: people will pay you, you can deliver, and the business model works. Everything after $1,000 is optimization. The FI community calls this the "ramen profitable" stage — your business covers its own costs and generates even a small surplus. From there, you compound both your skills and your revenue.

Side business income is the ultimate FI accelerator because it attacks both sides of the equation. Your day job covers expenses while 100% of your side income goes directly to investments. If your side business generates $1,500 per month and you invest all of it at 7% annual returns, you will have over $450,000 in 15 years. That is not a theoretical number — that is the real math of why thousands of FI seekers build side businesses.

One of the first decisions you face is how to structure your business legally. For most side businesses, the choice is between operating as a sole proprietorship (the default — no paperwork needed) or forming an LLC (Limited Liability Company). Here is the honest comparison.

A sole proprietorship is what you are by default when you start earning money on the side. There is no registration required at the federal level (though you may need a local business license). The income flows directly to your personal tax return on Schedule C. The downside: your personal assets — savings, home equity, investments — are not separated from business liabilities.

An LLC creates a legal separation between you and your business. If someone sues your business, your personal assets are generally protected. Formation costs range from $50 (states like Kentucky and Colorado) to $500 (California charges $800 per year just for the privilege of having an LLC). For most side businesses, an LLC is worth forming once you have paying customers.

Side business income is taxed differently than your W-2 paycheck, and the surprises can be painful if you are not prepared.

Self-employment tax: You pay both the employee AND employer portions of Social Security and Medicare — a combined 15.3% on top of your income tax. On W-2 income, your employer covers half. On side business income, you cover it all.

Quarterly estimated taxes: The IRS expects you to pay taxes as you earn, not once a year in April. If you owe more than $1,000 in taxes from side business income, you need to file quarterly estimated payments (Form 1040-ES) by April 15, June 15, September 15, and January 15.

Deductions that reduce your tax bill: Home office (simplified method: $5/sq ft up to 300 sq ft = $1,500 deduction), internet (business percentage), equipment, software subscriptions, mileage ($0.76 per mile in 2027), health insurance premiums (if self-employed), and retirement contributions (Solo 401k allows up to $75,000/year). These deductions are a significant FI advantage because they reduce both income tax AND self-employment tax.

Ready to unlock a world of free travel? Start with the Chase Sapphire Preferred® Card

ChooseFI has partnered with CardRatings for our coverage of credit card products. ChooseFI and CardRatings may receive a commission from card issuers.

The biggest constraint for side business owners is not money — it is time. You have 168 hours per week. Your day job takes 40-50. Sleep takes 49-56. That leaves 62-79 hours for everything else: family, health, friends, and your business. The key is not finding more time. It is being ruthlessly strategic about the time you have.

The 5-5-2 Framework: Dedicate 5 hours per weeknight (after work), 5 hours on Saturday, and 2 hours on Sunday to your business during the launch phase. That is 12 hours per week — enough to build meaningful momentum without destroying your health or relationships. After launch, you can often reduce to 5-8 hours per week for maintenance.

Batch your work. Do not context-switch between your day job and side business throughout the day. Dedicate specific blocks of time to specific tasks: Monday and Wednesday evenings for content creation, Tuesday and Thursday for customer communication, Saturday morning for strategic work.

The FI community offers a unique perspective on this question. Traditional business advice says to go full-time when your side income replaces your salary. The FI approach is more nuanced: your side business does not need to replace your income if your investments are covering the gap.

Consider going full-time when your side business income covers at least 75% of your essential expenses for 6 consecutive months, you have 12 months of expenses in cash reserves, your business is growing (not plateauing), and you have health insurance figured out independently of your employer.

But here is the FI twist: many people in the community deliberately keep their side business as a side business forever. A day job provides steady income, benefits, and 401(k) matching, while the side business provides additional investment capital. This "two-income engine" approach can cut your FI timeline dramatically. If your day job covers all expenses and your side business generates $2,000 per month in investable income, you add over $700,000 to your portfolio in 15 years at 7% returns.

The decision depends on your personal FI strategy. Some people want to build a business that replaces their job and provides fulfillment in early retirement. Others want to maximize total income during their accumulation phase and retire from everything. Both are valid paths — the important thing is making the choice intentionally with the math in front of you.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

From idea to first dollar with minimal risk.

Talk to 10 people who match your target customer profile. Describe your offer and ask if they would pay, and how much. Get at least 3 verbal commitments before building anything. If you cannot find 3 out of 10, pivot your idea.

Pro tip: Pre-sell before you build. Create a simple landing page describing your product or service and see if people will put down a deposit or join a waitlist.

Open a separate business bank account (free at most banks). File for an LLC in your state if you are ready for revenue. Get an EIN from the IRS (free, takes 5 minutes online). Set aside 30% of every dollar earned for taxes.

Pro tip: Do not commingle business and personal finances. A separate bank account makes tax time dramatically easier and protects your LLC liability shield.

Create the simplest version of your product or service that delivers value. A consultant needs a one-page website and a Calendly link. A product business needs one SKU, not twenty. A freelancer needs a portfolio with three examples. Launch ugly and improve based on real customer feedback.

Pro tip: Spend less than $200 on your MVP. If you need to spend more, you are overbuilding before you have proven demand.

Your first customers will come from your personal network, not from marketing. Tell everyone you know what you are building. Offer a founding-member discount in exchange for detailed feedback and a testimonial. The goal is revenue and learning, not profit.

Pro tip: Ask every customer two questions after delivery: "What was the best part?" and "What would you change?" These answers shape your product roadmap.

Once you have proven the model works, build systems so the business runs efficiently. Create templates, automate invoicing, document your processes. Find the acquisition channel that produces the best customers (referrals, content marketing, paid ads) and double down on it.

Pro tip: Track your effective hourly rate. If you earn $1,000 in a month and spend 20 hours, your rate is $50/hour. If you spend 80 hours, it is $12.50/hour — below minimum wage. Optimize for $/hour, not just total revenue.

The most powerful FI strategy is not choosing between your day job and a side business — it is running both simultaneously. Your day job covers all living expenses, employer benefits, and 401(k) matching. Your side business generates pure investment capital. If your side business produces $2,000 per month and you invest every dollar at 7% annual returns, you add over $700,000 to your portfolio in 15 years. That is the difference between retiring at 55 and retiring at 45.

Check your employment contract and company handbook for non-compete clauses, moonlighting policies, and intellectual property agreements. Many employers have no policy against side businesses as long as they do not compete, use company resources, or interfere with your job performance. If your contract has restrictions, consult an employment attorney before launching. Transparency is usually the safest approach if your side business is in a completely different industry.

Most service-based side businesses can launch for under $200: a domain name ($12/year), basic website hosting ($5-$15/month), and a business bank account (free). Product businesses may need $500-$2,000 for initial inventory. The FI approach is to minimize upfront investment and validate before spending. If your idea requires $10,000+ to test, consider whether there is a simpler version you can launch first.

Businesses with flexible hours and no geographic constraints work best: freelance writing, web development, consulting in your area of expertise, online tutoring, bookkeeping, virtual assistance, and digital product creation (courses, templates, ebooks). The key criteria are: work can be done on your schedule, no physical storefront needed, and revenue can grow without proportional time investment.

Report all income on Schedule C of your personal tax return. Pay self-employment tax (15.3%) plus income tax on profits. File quarterly estimated taxes using Form 1040-ES if you expect to owe $1,000 or more. Deduct legitimate business expenses: home office, internet (business %), equipment, software, mileage, and health insurance premiums. Consider a Solo 401(k) to shelter up to $75,000/year from taxes — this is one of the most powerful FI tools available to self-employed individuals.

Start as a sole proprietor during the validation phase (no cost, no paperwork). Form an LLC once you have paying customers and revenue. An LLC protects your personal assets (home, savings, investments) from business lawsuits and debts. Formation costs range from $50-$500 depending on your state. California charges $800/year minimum, which may not be worth it until you are earning at least $10,000/year from the business.

Dramatically. If your day job covers all expenses at a 50% savings rate, you reach FI in about 17 years. Add $2,000/month in side business income invested entirely, and your effective savings rate jumps well above 60% — cutting your FI timeline to roughly 12-13 years. At $3,000/month side income, you could reach FI in under 10 years from starting. The key insight: side business income has nearly 100% savings rate because your day job already covers expenses.

Starting a side business while working full-time is one of the most powerful financial moves you can make on the path to FI. Validate your idea before spending money, set up basic legal and tax infrastructure, launch an MVP quickly, and focus relentlessly on reaching your first $1,000 in revenue. The beauty of the side business approach is that your day job covers all expenses while your business income goes directly toward building wealth. Even modest side income of $1,000 to $2,000 per month, invested consistently over 10-15 years, can add hundreds of thousands of dollars to your portfolio and cut years off your timeline to financial independence.

#10166. AWS Lambda now supports GPU instances for Event Functions on Lambda Managed Instances

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

GPU support is now available for EC2 G-series GPU instances for Event Functions running on AWS Lambda Managed Instances (LMI). You can now run GPU-accelerated workloads, including machine learning inference, media processing, rendering, and scientific computing, on GPU instances with serverless simplicity. GPU-accelerated functions are deployed as container (Open Container Initiative, OCI) images. You build an OCI image, point the function at it, and let Lambda handle provisioning, scaling, and event integration. There are no GPU drivers, CUDA versions, or AMIs to reconcile, and no clusters or device plugins to operate. GPU-accelerated functions run on the same Lambda Managed Instances foundation that already gives you Amazon EC2 pricing and specialized compute without managing servers.

Why customers need this Customers keep asking to run GPU workloads with the simplicity of serverless. Building on self-managed GPU infrastructure means selecting instances, installing and reconciling GPU drivers and CUDA libraries, planning capacity, writing scaling logic, and hand-building the event integrations that connect the GPU step to the rest of the application. Serverless GPU services that hide that work often sit outside your AWS environment, with no native event sources, identity, or networking.

Cost is the second problem. GPUs are expensive, and utilization is usually low because a single workload rarely saturates a GPU. A small inference task can leave most of a provisioned GPU idle while you pay for the whole thing.

This feature closes both gaps. You bring a function, and Lambda runs it on a GPU in minutes, triggered by the same AWS events as any other Lambda Event Function, billed and secured the same way. It also maximizes utilization of every GPU you pay for, by consolidating functions onto instances and keeping GPUs busy across concurrent requests.

What you can build You can run both AI/ML inference and non-ML workloads using GPU-accelerated compute on Lambda Managed Instances. This capability is optimized for inference rather than training, and custom models rather than large foundation models already available on services like Amazon Bedrock. Common workloads include:

Part of the Lambda programming model A GPU-accelerated function is one step in a larger application, and it composes with the rest of Lambda. Lambda durable functions orchestrate multi-step, long-running workflows around the GPU step, with checkpointing and automatic recovery. Lambda MicroVMs run untrusted or model-generated code in isolated, stateful sandboxes. In an agentic workload, a MicroVM executes the tool-use logic on CPU, then calls a GPU-accelerated function running on Lambda Managed Instances for inference.

GPU-accelerated functions also support the extended function timeout on Lambda Managed Instances, up to 90 minutes for asynchronous and event source mapping invocations, so you can run batch and other long-running GPU jobs, such as media transcoding or multi-step inference, without re-architecting. GPU-accelerated functions support container images up to 32 GB (10 GB per file), up to 32 GB of ephemeral storage, 2,048 to 32,768 MB of memory, 3 to 48 GB of accelerator memory, up to 48 concurrent requests per execution environment, and up to 15,000 vCPUs per capacity provider.

Let’s try it out To show what this looks like in practice, I deployed an open-source large language model (LLM) from Hugging Face as a Lambda function and sent it a prompt. With the vLLM base image from Lambda, I only set the Hugging Face model ID in the MODEL_ID environment variable, without writing any inference code. I used Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, a small instruction-tuned model that suits in-application tasks such as summarization, classification, and extraction. The flow has four steps: create a capacity provider, build and push the container image, create a function from that image, then publish and invoke. Before you begin, request an increase to your G-series instance quota (listed as Running On-Demand G and VT instances), because new accounts might start with a quota of 0. For more information, see On-Demand Instance quotas.

First, I created a capacity provider, where I specify the GPU instance types Lambda can use and let it auto-scale. Listing several instance types lets Lambda pick the most cost-effective one that is available and fall back across generations when capacity is tight.

The Lambda console shows the new capacity provider, lmi-gpu-cp, in the Active state. Note the VPC configuration with its subnets and security group.

Figure 1: The capacity provider is Active, with its VPC configuration.

Next, I built the container image. Lambda provides a curated vLLM base image that includes the vLLM inference server, PyTorch, Transformers, and the CUDA toolkit, along with a runtime interface client that supports concurrent invocations. The image serves the model for me, so I don’t write a handler or any inference server code. My entire Dockerfile is one line:

After pushing the image to Amazon ECR, I created the function. I chose the model with the MODEL_ID environment variable, which accepts a Hugging Face or ModelScope repository, an Amazon S3 location, or a local path. Lambda downloads the model, manages GPU memory, and serves requests. Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct is a public model, so I didn’t need a Hugging Face token. For private or gated models, or to get higher download rate limits, you can also set HF_TOKEN.

GPU-accelerated functions use AcceleratorConfig to request accelerator memory. AcceleratorMemorySize is the minimum GPU memory the function needs, and Lambda places the execution environment on the smallest available GPU that meets it, so the GPU you get can have more memory than you requested. I set PerExecutionEnvironmentMaxConcurrency to 4, the default. The vLLM server batches concurrent requests on the GPU, so invocations share one copy of the model and the GPU stays busy. I also set ExecutionEnvironmentMemoryGiBPerVCpu to 4, which gives the function the 16,384 MB of memory and 4 vCPUs it needs while leaving capacity on the 2xlarge instance for Lambda’s management components.

The function overview shows what makes this a GPU-accelerated function: the attached capacity provider that supplies the GPU instances and the vLLM container image deployed from Amazon ECR, all wired into the same function model you already use.

Figure 2: The lmi-gpu-inference function overview, showing the attached capacity provider and the vLLM container image deployed from Amazon ECR.

Functions on Lambda Managed Instances run from a published version, so I published one. Publishing provisions the execution environments, and each one downloads the model weights and warms up the engine before the version becomes active. The cold start happens during the publish step, so the first caller doesn’t wait for it.

Then I sent the model a prompt. The payload is a standard OpenAI request body: a messages array routes to chat completions, and a prompt field routes to text completions.

The model answered from a GPU inside the Lambda execution environment, and the response is a standard OpenAI completion object, so any OpenAI-compatible client works with it.

Four concurrent invocations finished in about the same time as a single one, on one execution environment that shares one copy of the model in GPU memory. From a Hugging Face model ID to a live, OpenAI-compatible GPU endpoint took about 10 minutes, most of it unattended while Lambda provisioned capacity and warmed up the model.

That’s a working LLM endpoint with no inference server code, no GPU drivers, and no cluster to manage. To try a different model, I update MODEL_ID and publish a new version, with no image rebuild. Each version is an immutable snapshot, so different models can run side by side as separate versions. It is also a Lambda Event Function, so I can trigger it from an Amazon SQS queue to process documents in batches, put Amazon API Gateway in front of it, or call it as the inference step in a Lambda durable functions workflow.

For production, copy the model weights to an S3 bucket in the same Region, point MODEL_ID at that bucket, and set VLLM_LOAD_FORMAT=runai_streamer so the weights stream straight into memory. This gives faster starts and removes the dependency on the Hugging Face Hub. For more guidance, see the best practices for running inference workloads. Beyond LLM inference, you can also run custom ML inference, media processing, and other GPU workloads. See the Lambda documentation for best practices.

The configuration tab shows the settings that matter for a GPU-accelerated function: 16,384 MB of memory, the 15-minute timeout, and 32 GB of ephemeral storage for the downloaded weights, alongside the function-level capacity provider settings: per-environment concurrency of 4, the 4:1 memory-to-vCPU ratio, and 16 GB of accelerator memory.

Figure 3: The function’s configuration, including memory, timeout, and 32 GB of ephemeral storage, and the function-level capacity provider settings: per-environment concurrency, the memory-to-vCPU ratio, and accelerator memory.

When you finish testing, delete the function and then the capacity provider so that Lambda stops the GPU instances and you stop paying for them.

How it works The capabilities below are what you get out of the box, each one removing GPU work you would otherwise own.

Skip the GPU software setup. Curated base images ship with the frameworks and tools handled for you, so you do not install or reconcile them: base:cuda (CUDA toolkit and runtime), cupy:cuda (CuPy, NumPy, SciPy, Pandas, Numba), pytorch:cuda (PyTorch, torchvision, torchaudio, transformers, FFmpeg), and serving images vllm:cuda and sglang:cuda. Use them directly, extend them, or bring a fully custom image.

No GPU infrastructure to operate. Lambda handles driver compatibility, provisioning, routing, and load balancing, so you ship functions instead of running GPU fleets. Deploy with the same Console, CLI, AWS CloudFormation, AWS CDK, AWS SAM, and Terraform workflow as any Lambda Event Function.

Trigger GPU work from any AWS event. The same 200+ event sources that trigger Event Functions, including Amazon S3, Amazon SQS, Amazon API Gateway, and Amazon EventBridge, trigger GPU-accelerated functions directly, with the same scaling, error handling, and retries. No custom integration code.

Serve concurrent requests on each GPU. Each execution environment can process multiple concurrent invocations on the same GPU, which you control with the per-environment concurrency setting. Raising it packs more requests onto each GPU instance to drive utilization up, so you get more throughput from the capacity you already pay for.

Multiple instance types. Specify multiple instance types or generations, and Lambda selects the most cost-effective available option and falls back across your list to reduce Insufficient Capacity Errors.

Capacity options. GPU instances run as Amazon EC2 On-Demand instances, and your existing Savings Plans and Reserved Instances apply. For predictable GPU availability, create an EC2 On-Demand Capacity Reservation (ODCR) that matches your capacity provider configuration, and Lambda uses that reserved capacity automatically.

Large container images and storage. Container images up to 32 GB are supported, along with up to 32 GB of NVMe ephemeral storage in /tmp. Seekable OCI (SOCI) lazy loading lets your function begin executing before the full image is downloaded, which reduces execution environment startup time by up to 70%.

See GPU usage without extra setup. GPU utilization, GPU memory utilization, GPU memory used, and GPU power utilization appear alongside standard function metrics in Amazon CloudWatch and in the AWS Lambda console.

After invoking the function, the console surfaces the GPU-specific metrics next to the standard ones. Track GPU utilization, GPU memory utilization, GPU memory used, and GPU power utilization to see how much of the accelerator each invocation actually uses, which is the signal that drives both auto-scaling and cost.

Figure 4: GPU utilization, GPU memory utilization, GPU memory used, and GPU power utilization for the vLLM function under concurrent load.

Pricing and availability This feature is generally available today, supporting the Amazon EC2 G-series (G4dn, G5, G6, G6f, Gr6, Gr6f, G6e) at launch, with more instance families to come. You pay the underlying EC2 instance fee (eligible for Savings Plans and Reserved Instances), plus a 9.75 percent management fee for GPU instances, and the standard Lambda request fee of $0.20 per million requests. For pricing and Region availability, see AWS Lambda pricing.

You can also build with your AI coding assistants and agents: explore the Builder Center and connect your tools with the Agent Toolkit for AWS, which bundles the AWS MCP Server and curated agent skills.

To get started, visit the AWS Lambda console and see the GPU-accelerated functions documentation.

Daniel Abib is a senior specialist solutions architect at AWS, focused on generative AI and Amazon Bedrock — and passionate about serverless. He helps startups and enterprises build AI-powered applications and modernize with cloud-native architectures. A three-time Ironman finisher and four-time re:Invent speaker, he brings the same endurance mindset to building cloud solutions.

#10164. F-Secure Identifies 26 Ways AI Can Harm Consumers, and Where to Stop Them

New AI Harms Taxonomy traces how harm moves through AI systems, from misleading chatbot answers to compromised autonomous agents, and identifies where defenses can intervene.

LOS ANGELES (November 10, 2026) — AI now shapes how we search, communicate, decide and run our digital lives. But when it goes wrong, how does it actually hurt people? F-Secure’s new AI Harms Taxonomy maps 26 distinct harms to consumers, tracing the path each one takes to reach a person. The result is a clear view of where protections can step in, and who is in the best position to help.

Developed by Dr. Megan Squire, Principal Threat Intelligence Researcher at F-Secure, the taxonomy tackles a blind spot in how AI risk is discussed. Most frameworks list what could potentially go wrong, but stop short of connecting those harms to the systems and companies that could prevent them. F-Secure’s research goes further by mapping every layer between an AI-enabled threat and its impact on a consumer, then pinpointing where defenses need to go.

“As AI systems become more capable, the consequences of their mistakes can extend well beyond a bad answer,” said Dr. Squire. “The people building them have a responsibility to understand how they can fail and who gets hurt when they do, and to put safeguards in place before that harm happens.”

Related:Critical PHP Flaws Allow Code Execution, but Don't Panic 'PHP2Shell'-Style

The taxonomy reflects how the risks themselves are changing. It used to be a criminal going after a person directly, but now AI sits in the middle, which adds new players and new ways for things to go wrong. An assistant acting on someone’s behalf can be manipulated into doing damage, and a chatbot can mislead with complete confidence. Underneath, the trouble often starts in how an AI handles instructions and memory, or in the other services it’s connected to.

The research currently maps 26 harms across five relationship types and 19 intermediary layers, with 24 defenses designed to address risks at different points. Examples range from AI-generated voice and video impersonation to agents making harmful financial decisions, exposing personal information or acting on outdated context. It also examines attacks in which malicious instructions are hidden in content an AI system processes, potentially influencing what an agent does or what a chatbot tells a user.

One of the framework’s central findings is that responsibility for preventing AI harm is spread across several parties. Some risks can be handled by security software on a person’s own device. Others have to be dealt with further upstream, by the companies that build AI products and the systems behind them, including how an AI remembers things and which other services it connects to. Sorting out which is which makes it clear where a defense belongs and who has to build it. It also undercuts the idea that people can stay safe on their own just by spotting suspicious content or double-checking what an AI tells them.

Related:Cursor Traces Malicious Claude Code Extensions to Third-Party Breach

This question of who’s responsible gets more urgent as AI moves from producing information to acting on it. A chatbot that gets an answer wrong can mislead someone, but at least that person is part of the exchange. An agent with access to a person’s accounts and the services linked to them can cause real consequences without that kind of back-and-forth. Protections need to be built around what these systems actually do, which starts with understanding how the two differ.

This research is part of a wider effort at F-Secure to build trust into the AI era. F-Secure Trust, announced alongside a strategic alliance with Quantum AI Lab QuTwo, is the company’s work on protecting people in a world where AI agents increasingly act for them. TrustGuard is designed to help protect people during AI interactions, while TrustPath aims to secure their digital journeys from discovery to delivery. The AI Harms Taxonomy gives that work a research foundation by spelling out the risks the protections need to address, and it makes clear that meaningful safeguards also depend on the companies building and running AI systems.

Related:Grok Bots Attempt to Access Corporate Data Without User Approval

Today’s digital threats go far beyond just scams. F-Secure’s Illuminate Research team studies people and technology as they actually interact, exploring the messy realities of trust, deception, and online resilience in the AI era.

The AI Harms Taxonomy is available on the Illuminate Research Hub.

F-Secure is a human-first, AI-powered consumer cyber security experience company with 38 years of expertise in tackling digital threats. We help digital service providers turn trust into a high-value growth engine — protecting their customers while enabling them to live their best digital lives in a world of relentless, AI-driven scams. With billions of digital interactions secured each year, tens of millions of consumers protected globally, and over $10bn in partner value created, we deliver proven impact at scale.

#10162. AWS Weekly Roundup: Local Zone in Bogotá, 30-minute Lambda timeout, and more (November 24, 2026)

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

Some limits shape architecture more than any feature does. AWS Lambda launched in 2014 with a 60-second timeout, moved to 5 minutes in 2015 and to 15 minutes in 2018, and then stayed there for eight years. Last week the limit doubled, and in the same week Lambda learned to run on GPUs and gave platform teams their own runtimes. That is a lot of Lambda for one week.

Two other threads ran through the week. A new AWS Local Zone in Bogotá brought AWS infrastructure closer to users in Colombia. And AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS), Amazon Cognito, and Amazon CloudFront each shipped a piece of identity for agents: who an agent is when it shows up at your door, and how little it should be allowed to do. Now, let’s get into this week’s AWS news…

HeadlinesAWS Local Zone in Bogotá, Colombia – AWS opened a new Local Zone in Bogotá, so you can now run applications that need single-digit millisecond latency closer to users in Colombia. AWS Local Zones place compute, storage, and networking near large metropolitan areas with the same APIs and tools as a Region, for workloads like real-time gaming, live streaming, and financial transactions. The Bogotá Local Zone supports Amazon EC2 C7i, M7i, and R7i instances, Amazon EBS gp3, gp2, and io1 volumes with local snapshots, Amazon S3 One Zone-Infrequent Access, Amazon ECS, Amazon EKS, AWS Direct Connect, and Application Load Balancer. To start, enable us-east-1-bog-1a in AWS Global View.

AWS Lambda now supports a 30-minute timeout for on-demand Event Functions – You can now configure any Event Function with a timeout of up to 30 minutes (1,800 seconds), double the previous limit, for synchronous, asynchronous, and event source mapping invocations. On Lambda Managed Instances, synchronous invocations also go to 30 minutes, while asynchronous and event source mapping invocations keep the 90-minute timeout from September. Standard Lambda pricing applies, in all commercial Regions. Agentic requests that chain model and tool calls, media transcoding, and file conversion are the workloads AWS calls out. Before raising the value, read the considerations in the Compute blog post: a service that invokes your function synchronously applies its own timeout, client read and socket timeouts need to be longer, and a NAT gateway drops idle connections after 350 seconds, so long calls need keep-alives. For longer work, durable functions still run for up to a year, and now emit OpenTelemetry traces.

AWS Lambda now supports GPU instances for Event Functions on Lambda Managed Instances – You can now run GPU-accelerated inference, media processing, rendering, and scientific computing as Lambda functions, packaged as OCI container images, with no GPU drivers, CUDA versions, or AMIs to reconcile. In the walkthrough, four concurrent requests shared one copy of the model on a single GPU and finished in about the same time as one. It’s optimized for inference with custom models, on G-series instances from G4dn to G6e. You pay the EC2 instance fee (Savings Plans and Reserved Instances apply), a 9.75% management fee for GPU instances, and $0.20 per million requests.

Last week’s launches Here are some launches and updates from this past week that caught my attention:

Amazon Polly now supports voice cloning – You can now create a custom voice in Amazon Polly from a few minutes of recordings of a consenting speaker, running on the Polly generative engine. It launches in seven languages, including English, Spanish, and Portuguese, and each cloned voice speaks the language it was recorded in. Polly doesn’t keep the recordings or train on them. Creating a voice is free, and you pay for the speech you synthesize. It’s a gated release, so request access through your account team. The ML blog post covers brand-voice use cases for contact centers, voice agents, and content.

AWS Lambda now supports customer-managed runtimes – You can now manage a Lambda runtime as its own resource, separate from function deployment. Platform teams package approved SDKs, certificates, and security or monitoring agents as an OCI image in Amazon ECR, and functions pick up updates as Lambda recycles execution environments, with no per-function redeploys. Updates can be staged, with health metrics and rollback. There’s no additional charge, and the Compute blog walkthrough shows the platform-team workflow.

AWS STS announces GetScopedCredentials to scope down permissions at runtime – You can now take the temporary credentials a workload already holds and derive narrower ones, cutting permissions, session duration, or both. The result keeps the caller’s original identity, and permissions only ever narrow, so a sub-agent can never end up with more access than whoever created it. Access to the API is controlled with IAM policies, and every call lands in AWS CloudTrail. It’s available in all commercial Regions and beyond, including GovCloud (US) and China.

Amazon Cognito now supports dynamic client registration – You can now let MCP clients and AI agents register themselves at runtime (RFC 7591) instead of creating an app client in advance for each one. You choose between requiring an access token with a registration scope or opening registration to any client, and dynamically registered clients carry a client ID prefix so you can filter and manage them with the rest. It’s available on the Essentials and Plus plans, and client ID metadata documents arrived the same week.

Amazon CloudFront Functions now supports network calls – You can now call your own APIs from a function with cf.fetch() and act on the response, so authorization, personalization, or fraud checks that used to need Lambda@Edge or a trip to the origin can run at the edge. AWS’s own example: check an AI agent’s permissions and usage budget before the request reaches your API or MCP server. Network calls cost $0.10 per million on top of invocation charges and are included in the Pro flat-rate plan and above. The Networking blog post also covers cf.beacon() for background analytics.

Amazon Aurora DSQL now supports multi-Region peering across continents – You can now build a multi-Region Amazon Aurora DSQL cluster with peers on different continents, such as US East (N. Virginia) and Europe (Ireland), and read and write to one strongly consistent database from both. It was a busy week for DSQL: PL/pgSQL functions and procedures arrived, and storage upgrades brought up to 50% higher write throughput at no additional cost.

For a full list of AWS announcements, be sure to keep an eye on the What’s New with AWS page.

Other AWS news Here are some additional posts and resources that you might find interesting:

How the BMW Group governs hundreds of agents across thousands of developers – With more than 12,500 developers in R&D, BMW treats governance as deterministic code outside the agent’s reasoning loop, mostly in the CI/CD pipeline, so neither the agent nor its prompts can override it.

AI coding agents on Amazon EC2 Mac: How Cognition scales Devin for macOS – Cognition starts a fresh macOS VM for each Devin session in under a minute on Amazon EC2 Mac hosts across six Regions. The detail worth stealing: sessions are decoupled from hosts, and Cognition reports that about 90% release their VM when idle and resume from a snapshot in seconds.

How Klaviyo processes 3 billion daily events at scale with Amazon MSK Express – Timely reading for Black Friday week. Klaviyo moved from RabbitMQ to Amazon MSK Express brokers to absorb roughly 7 times normal event volume during Black Friday and Cyber Monday. Running one set of processors across three priority topics, instead of separate infrastructure per level, cut costs by roughly 30%, according to the team.

Upcoming AWS events Check your calendar and sign up for upcoming AWS events:

AWS re:Invent – It’s next week: join the community in Las Vegas from November 30 to December 4, or register for the free livestream to follow the keynotes and selected sessions remotely.

AWS Community Days – Find community-led technical talks, workshops, and hands-on learning near you. Check each organizer’s page for current dates and registration details.

Join the AWS Builder Center to connect with builders, share solutions, and access content that supports your development. Browse here for upcoming AWS-led in-person and virtual events and developer-focused events.

That’s all for this week. Check back next Monday for another Weekly Roundup!

This post is part of our Weekly Roundup series. Check back each week for a quick roundup of interesting news and announcements from AWS!

#10156. Nvidia’s journey to forge the one CPU SKU to rule them all

Each generation of Intel Xeons or AMD Epycs brings dozens of new SKUs: high-core count parts tuned for throughput, high-frequency ones optimized for latency, low-power chips designed for the edge, and everything in between.

But if you ask Ian Finder, who heads development of Nvidia’s datacenter CPUs, for the vast majority of use cases, all those SKUs are overkill. “There was sort of an overskewing, for many years on the other vendors side of things,” he told El Reg on the sidelines of Supercomputing 2026 this week.

Nvidia is a relative newcomer to the datacenter CPU space. The company’s first part, called Grace, launched in 2023, but didn’t see widespread deployment outside AI headnodes, high-end NICs, and a handful of standalone CPU deployments by hyperscalers like Meta.

We explored the chip in greater detail earlier this year, but the GPU slinger’s latest CPU, Vera, is a different beast. In addition to showing up as part of Nvidia’s Vera-Rubin superchips, the CPU will also be offered in standalone CPU racks, storage, and context memory systems. Just this week, Nvidia announced a new rack with 192 Vera CPUs aimed at high-performance computing (HPC) workloads, which don’t translate well to GPUs. And at least on paper, a single SKU with 88 cores clocking to 3.3 GHz powers all of them.

Historically, CPUs with different combinations of cores, clocks, I/O, and power have been used in these roles.

According to Finder, most datacenter CPUs haven’t been loaded that heavily, and as a result unloaded latency has been the optimization point. As a consequence, CPUs that rely on high core counts to boost throughput often suffer from elevated loaded latencies.

On the flip side, chipmakers can optimize for loaded latency, but this usually comes at the expense of throughput. We see this pretty clearly across AMD’s last few generations of Epycs, where it designed both space-efficient Zen-C cores that prioritize throughput and fatter frequency-optimized versions that prioritize latency.

“As more cores are up and running, your latency to memory gets higher because you have to deal with congestion on the connectivity buses,” More than Moore analyst Ian Cutress tells The Reg. “What Nvidia is saying is right. Sometimes, you do have to pick between core count and core latency.”

88 custom cores, 176 funky threads, 1.5 TB of laptop RAM, and 1.8 TB/s of NVLink connectivity — this isn't your typical datacenter chip

With Vera, Nvidia sought to thread that needle to design a balanced architecture that maintains low latencies and high throughput even when all 88 cores are loaded. This does mean making some compromises – in particular, the CPU's vector unit isn’t the beefiest. But if you’ve got GPUs capable of running vectorized code, that compromise starts to make a lot more sense – and if you’re buying Vera you almost certainly do.

“If you do a few things like that, you can actually get a part that, for the workloads people care about — knowledge agents for CI/CD, for software engineering, or databases and analytics — you can build a part that gets you the same performance per core as the frequency-optimized x86 parts at the same throughput per watt as the density-optimized parts,” Finder argued.

We should note that Nvidia isn’t unique in this line of thinking. Cloud providers have long passed on the highest core-count parts in Intel or AMD’s lineup in favor of parts they feel deliver the right mix of throughput, latency, memory bandwidth, and capacity.

The reason, in part, is that cloud providers don’t know exactly what their customers will actually be doing with those cores, and so it’s better to offer something that’s well-rounded than one that has the highest clocks or lowest latency. This same logic works pretty well for agent sandboxes.

“Nvidia is confident that it understands the market well enough that one chip will cover most use cases,” Cutress said. “You could argue that Nvidia just wants to corner that bulk market.”

Having said that, there are plenty of reasons to have multiple SKUs or product lines. Throughout much of AMD’s latest launch cycle, the House of Zen has argued its chips make for better agentic CPUs because agents aren’t one workload but many. Some may benefit from higher core counts while others may benefit from higher frequency, and AMD’s more diverse portfolio offers choice.

To that point, Vera does have one trick up its sleeve: a split personality. The processor is one of the few Arm chips that supports simultaneous multithreading (SMT), but the way it works differs wildly from what Intel would call Hyperthreading.

When enabled, SMT effectively bifurcates Vera’s Olympus cores. Eighty-eight cores turns into something that functionally looks more like 176 narrower cores. So if you need more throughput than 88 cores afford, or your workload doesn’t benefit materially from a wider core, Vera does offer some flexibility.

16 channels of DDR5, 5 GHz clock speeds, and support for CXL 3.1 memory god boxes? What's not to like?

A broader SKU stack also helps with binning. Chip manufacturing isn’t a perfect process, and you’re going to run into defective cores from time to time. Binning allows chipmakers to sell these defective chips at a discount.

Cutress suspects that Nvidia is probably doing this for its customers and it’s just not advertising them as different SKUs the way Intel and AMD have the past few decades.

For instance, a hyperscaler might be happy to take a 72-core variant of Vera that clocks a little higher or delivers more memory bandwidth per core for the same amount of power.

But for now we have just the one, for the green lord on their green throne in the land of AI where the bots lie. ®

Easy-to-use, popular, not collecting data for us, or serving our ads? Down with you!

PARTNER CONTENT: PARTNER CONTENT: Hyperscalers and colos have distinct power strategies, but for both, securing flexible, fast power relies on self-generated solutions

No sophisticated malware or attack framework required

#10154. How to handle user, connector, and agent auth in Gemini Enterprise with a custom UI

When using the out-of-the-box Gemini Enterprise web application, identity and access management happen behind the scenes. Employees sign in through Google Identity or Workforce Identity Federation (such as Microsoft Entra ID or Okta), search results automatically respect per-user access controls (ACLs), and third-party login prompts for Federated Connectors or custom agents appear automatically in the chat UI.

In practice, however, many enterprises build their own Customer UI to embed Gemini Enterprise into an internal portal, intranet, or line-of-business application. To do this, Customer BE calls the Gemini Enterprise StreamAssist API.

The moment an application builds a custom UI and calls the StreamAssist API directly, the three authentication flows that the built-in web app handled automatically become the responsibility of Customer UI and Customer BE:

This guide walks through the architecture, component flows, and runnable code for all three layers (Figure 1):

Depending on whether Customer UI is acting on behalf of an individual employee or running as a shared backend bot, customer architectures follow three keyless authentication patterns:

Required IAM Role and License: Every principal calling the StreamAssist API must be granted the Discovery Engine User IAM role on the GCP project. For human user patterns (Pattern 1A and Pattern 1B), the user (or Workforce Pool group) must also have an active Gemini Enterprise user license assigned in the Admin Console.

When employees sign in with Google Workspace or Cloud Identity and the Gemini Enterprise engine is configured for Google Identity, the flow has zero extra hops:

When employees sign into Customer UI with Microsoft Entra ID or Okta and Gemini Enterprise uses Workforce Identity Federation (WIF), you reuse the same Workforce Pool that is already connected to Gemini Enterprise:

When a backend service, Slack/Teams webhook, or non-personalized knowledge bot needs to call StreamAssist without any Service Account JSON keys, use a project-scoped Workload Identity Pool:

When to use Pattern 1C vs. Patterns 1A and 1B: Because Workload Identity Federation authenticates a machine rather than an individual human employee, Gemini Enterprise sees every request as coming from the same workload identity. Use Pattern 1C for non-personalized bots over shared knowledge bases, and use Pattern 1A or Pattern 1B whenever you need per-user document ACLs, user-specific 3LO connectors, or restricted custom agents.

Once Customer UI and Customer BE are calling StreamAssist as the signed-in employee, users can query native Federated Connectors (such as Google Drive, Microsoft SharePoint Online, Jira, Box, ServiceNow, and Confluence) that search live third-party APIs in real time.

Because Federated Connectors enforce user-level permissions in the third-party system, each employee authorizes the connector once using 3-Legged OAuth (3LO). Gemini Enterprise stores those per-user refresh tokens inside its own managed Keystore, so Customer BE never has to store third-party credentials in its own database.

Note for Custom UIs: Enabling Automatic Popup Handoff from GE FE Server: All Gemini Enterprise Federated Connectors use Google's managed /oauth-redirect endpoint on GE FE Server as their fixed callback URL. By default, that page only sends browser postMessage events to the built-in Gemini Enterprise web app origin. When deploying Customer UI on a custom domain, work with your Gemini Enterprise team to enable the custom origin flag so Step 7 automatically posts the callback URL to Customer UI and closes the popup.

Native Federated Connectors are ideal for federated search and built-in actions. When a workflow requires multi-step reasoning, custom Python logic, or orchestrating across multiple enterprise APIs in a single assistant, teams build a custom ADK agent, deploy it on Vertex AI Agent Engine, and register it in Gemini Enterprise.

When registering an ADK agent in Gemini Enterprise, attaching static OAuth configurations directly to the root agent registration creates two issues for a custom UI:

To trigger 3-Legged OAuth on demand per tool and let Gemini Enterprise and Google Cloud manage the per-user Token Vault automatically, follow three simple setup steps:

#10152. Signal slams door on prying AI agents to protect privacy

Apple, Google, and Microsoft called out for failing to provide developers with adequate AI agent controls

Citing the screenshot blocking implemented last year to defend against Microsoft Recall, Signal President Meredith Whittaker said similar measures have become necessary to prevent agentic software available in iOS and Android from gaining access to communication that's intended to be private.

"Google and Apple are integrating agentic capabilities like Gemini Intelligence with Google Gemini and Onscreen Awareness with Siri AI deep into their operating systems," wrote Whittaker in a blog post.

"In order to gain access to information about you, these OS AI agents 'scan' the information that’s on your screen. In doing so, your Signal data is captured, then made available to these agentic systems in order to allow them to answer your questions and do your bidding."

The result is that private messages ingested by these agentic systems may end up on someone else's server where they're no longer private.

Updates to Signal's iOS and Android apps now include controls that by default block Siri on iOS and Gemini and other agents on Android from taking screenshots, which AI agents process using optical character recognition to extract text.

Whittaker says that those who wish to take screenshots of their messages can disable this protection via Signal's settings menu, but warns doing so risks exposing your Signal content (and that sent by your contacts) to AI agents.

Screenshot blocking, Whittaker concedes, is only a partial solution since AI agents may have other ways to access application data through the device file system.

"Even where we are able to detect and block AI data access, Google, Apple, Microsoft, and others do not provide ways for developers to easily restrict such AI data access without making drastic compromises to the core user experience. This is also the reason we are currently unable to implement these protections on macOS."

Apple is taking some action to implement protection from AI agents on macOS, though it appears to be doing so largely to defend against third-party access to data rather than to limit its own Siri service. Siri can be turned off separately.

With macOS 27.3, currently in public beta testing, Apple's desktop operating system will get more granular controls on two APIs: Device Control and Data Access, and Full Disk Access. The result will be more permission prompts for users of macOS apps that seek access to user data, and the inconvenience of applying for an entitlement from Apple to obtain Full Disk Access for backup software and assistive technologies that use Accessibility APIs.

Apple is also reportedly working on its own AI agent in a bid to compete with Anthropic and OpenAI. It remains to be seen whether Apple will use its operating system ownership as competitive leverage against AI firms that threaten its gatekeeping power, as it has done with web browsers.

While Apple casts itself as an ally that's helping to defend device customers against prying AI agents, Signal's decision to block Siri shows that AI agents have complicated trust decisions and the competitive landscape. AI agents are supposed to serve the user but when they spirit data away to the cloud and come between customers and merchants, they wear out their welcome.

AI agents also threaten the business relationship between consumers and merchants.

Amazon has blocked shopping agents like Meta Muse from its e-commerce site (though not AWS Marketplace) while Walmart has invited third-party agents to interact through its AI Gateway. Anthropic has blocked subscription customers from using third-party harnesses. United Airlines has banned transactions from unauthorized AI bots. Similar barriers or limitations related to third-party AI agents have been erected by Target, Home Depot, Etsy, eBay, JPMorgan Chase, Charles Schwab, Citigroup, and Wells Fargo, among others.

Meanwhile, there's a burgeoning industry in bot detection and mitigation, led by the likes of Cloudflare, Akamai, and DataDome, to name a few.

Now AI agents face the same scrutiny as people wonder whether their agents really serve them or someone else. After a year or two of agent vendors arguing for access to all digital data for the sake of personalization and context, the backlash has arrived. ®

Correction: An earlier version of this article misstated Meredith Whitaker's title. She is the president of the Signal Foundation.

#10147. Visa, Mastercard theft included info useful for phishing, but not draining bank accounts, say researchers

No financial details, but plenty of other clues

Visa and Mastercard customer info leaked by the data thieves and extortionists who claim to have stolen it from the financial giants doesn’t include card numbers, passwords, or home addresses.

On Thursday, Visa, Mastercard, and JPMorgan Chase confirmed to The Register that they are investigating third-party breaches that may involve customer information.

Ransomware and extortion crew n0n claimed to be responsible for all three, and on Thursday published samples of the stolen files – data-theft groups typically leak some of the compromised data ahead of the official extortion deadline in an effort to put more pressure on victim companies to pay the ransom demand.

Researchers at SOCRadar, a threat-intelligence firm that has been tracking n0n since it emerged in September, have parsed all 14 files in the n0n’s published Visa and Mastercard archives, and told us that the files don’t contain any financial details belonging to banking customers.

“We found no card numbers, expiry dates, CVVs, passwords, dates of birth, or home addresses in any file,” Ensar Seker, SOCRadar chief information security officer, told The Register. “Visa's statement that no card numbers were affected matches our scan.”

Mastercard previously said that stolen data included a “limited amount of email addresses – non-payments information” from a subset of cardholders in a benefits program.

According to SOCRadar’s analysis, the leaked files do contain mobile numbers, Bank Identification Number (BIN) prefixes, and dated airline, hotel, and restaurant bookings. So while the data doesn’t include card or account numbers that crooks could use to drain customers' bank accounts or rack up massive credit card bills, “that detail is what a fraudster needs to pose as a concierge desk or a bank,” in an email or voice-phishing social engineering attack, Seker said.

It’s also worth noting that the published files only represent about 0.4 percent of the data n0n claims to have hoovered up in the breach: about 4.3 GB and 1.9 million marketing events. “So a further release is possible,” according to Seker.

Plus, both datasets come from the same upstream provider, we’re told. They use the same 27-column booking layout, placeholder text,  and CRM ID format, Seker said, noting that the most recent record in each stops 69 seconds apart on Oct 26.

“This looks like two card programs run on one provider's platform,” he said. “The pattern looks like a batch copy from a marketing and reporting store.”

The security shop hasn’t analyzed the Chase customer data that n0n also claimed to have published.

All three financial firms attributed the compromises to a third-party provider, and while nothing in the stolen files name the vendor, the timing matches up with a Qubole supply-chain campaign disclosed earlier this month by Wiz, working with Google's Mandiant incident responders and AI data security company Sentra.

In that attack, which the threat hunters said began on October 14, cybercrims used compromised identity and access management (IAM) credentials associated with Qubole's AWS account to access dozens of downstream-customers’ environments, stealing secrets and other sensitive data from S3 buckets.

N0n also claimed to have carried out this attack. “The timing fits the Qubole supply-chain campaign,” Seker said, adding that this is his company’s assessment, not a confirmed finding. "If it's right, Qubole would be the access path rather than the holder of the data, and the files point to a concierge-services operator behind the brands.”

Qubole’s parent company Idera did not respond to The Register’s inquiries. The financial institutions declined to answer our questions about which provider(s) was compromised in the breach, and whether Qubole was the access path by which their customers’ data was exposed.®

#10141. PHP 8.6: Things are gonna get a lot more strict around here

Like a parent disciplining a misbehaving child, PHP’s annual update clamps down on loose default behaviors to curb malicious hacker horseplay.

On Thursday,  PHP version 8.6 was released on its usual yearly schedule, popping up as if the web scripting language hadn’t been under intense scrutiny this past month.

For those who missed it: a swarm of unearthed vulnerabilities has plagued PHP since October, allowing unauthenticated attackers to crash PHP workers or execute arbitrary code on the server. Users of every PHP-based application, including WordPress, Laravel, and Symfony, had to scramble to update their PHP instances.

The PHP core development community, along with the PHP Foundation, responded with a coordinated release of patched versions of PHP (8.5.13, 8.4.28, 8.3.37, 8.2.36 and the new 8.6) that closed seven critical remote code execution (RCE) flaws in one sweep.

In addition to patching specific exploitable holes in the PHP engine, the new version of PHP also tamps down some of PHP’s historically liberal configuration settings.

Lucky for PHP, and for the rest of us, core developer Jörg Sowa called out these lax settings in June, when he posted a proposal to set more security-friendly defaults.

Sowa saw that three configuration settings of PHP’s session extension – Strict Mode, HttpOnly, and SameSite – were designed to boost security but shipped “with defaults that leave applications unnecessarily exposed.”

In a retrospective posted last month, the PHP Foundation admitted that consensus-driven gridlock had stalled complex fixes for months and vowed to get patches out the door sooner, should the vulnerabilities leak ahead of the agreed-upon deadline.

Code-wise, Sowa’s proposal to harden PHP settings was accepted for PHP 8.6. These changes won’t stop the flood of AI-generated CVEs, but at least these holes will be more difficult to exploit.

For Strict Mode, PHP will no longer accept uninitialized session IDs that it didn’t create itself. Before, an attacker could just plant a made-up session ID on the victim to gain entry. “The built-in files handler checks whether a session file for that ID exists on disk; because an attacker-planted ID has no corresponding file, a fresh random ID is generated instead,” Sowa wrote.

Collectively, these changes should thwart session hijacking and cross-site request forgeries. They won’t stop remote code execution hacks, though they will make it a lot more difficult for attackers to gain entry.

The new release brings some other security-minded changes as well.

The PHP engine will be a lot stricter when it encounters strings with null values (“\0”). In many cases, it will now throw an error rather than silently letting it pass. Null values are a favored trick for triggering RCEs. A C function will treat the null value as the end of the string, processing everything before the null byte and ignoring everything after, much to an attacker’s delight.

Continuing in this spirit of “if you see something, say something,” a number of other PHP functions will also no longer remain silent when things go awry. Operations for closing a file or copying data, for instance, will explicitly return a false value when terminating prematurely or failing, an alert developers could use to minimize data integrity bugs.

Beyond security, PHP 8.6 also comes with a number of new goodies.

PHPers are abuzz over Partial Function Application (PFA), which will let them pre-fill some of a function’s arguments to generate a reusable closure on the fly — leapfrogging JavaScript in this ability.

The new edition also gets a native polling API, which will be handy for event-driven networking; a stopwatch-like Duration class that can track elapsed time across more than 250 years; and a clamp() function to ensure a given value stays within an acceptable range.

For administrators, this PHP update round may be a bit of a shambles.

As backend software engineer Rajekevin Dourepoulle notes in a blog post, these changes will take effect post-installation only if the configurations aren’t specified elsewhere, such as in an application, framework, or even another php.ini file – all of which can override the PHP settings themselves.

“In practice: if your deployment reuses the old php.ini (an Ansible template, a COPY php.ini in a Dockerfile, a file copied from server to server), you move to 8.6 and gain nothing, because the explicit session.use_strict_mode = 0 line overrides the new default,” he wrote.

Despite all these heroic efforts, PHP remains the champion of inscrutability. ®
